<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We construct a counterexample to Kaplansky's direct-finiteness conjecture in odd characteristic. For one specified odd prime <i>p</i>, we obtain a field <i>K</i> of order <i>p</i><sup>4</sup>, a finitely generated group <i>G</i> containing torsion, and finite sums $`a,b\in K[G]`$ with $`ab=1`$ but $`ba\ne1`$. The same elements define a cellular automaton on <i>K</i><sup><i>G</i></sup> that is injective but not surjective.

- **claim-main-counterexample** (counterexample, [source](#stmt-op-main)): Put $$m=\binom{1200}{600},\qquad p=\min\{h\in\mathbb Z:h>1,\ h\mid(m!)^2+1\}.$$ Then $p$ is an odd prime. There are a specified field $K$ of order $p^4$, a specified finitely generated group $G$ containing torsion, and specified finite sums $a,b\in K[G]$ satisfying $ab=1$ and $ba\ne1$.
  - Explanation: For one specified odd prime p, defined from a central binomial coefficient m, the theorem gives a field of order p^4, a finitely generated group with torsion, and finite sums a and b with ab=1 and ba not equal to 1. It settles only the displayed prime.
- **claim-field-tower** (construction, [source](#stmt-op-field)): For $m,p$ in Theorem <a href="#op:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:main">1</a>, $p>m$ is an odd prime and $-1$ is a square in $\mathbb F_p$. If $\eta$ is a nonsquare in $\mathbb F_p^*$, then $$K_0=\mathbb F_p[\xi]/(\xi^2-\eta),\qquad K=K_0[\nu]/(\nu^2-\xi)$$ are quadratic field extensions, so $\|K\|=p^4$.
  - Explanation: The prime exceeds m, is odd, and -1 is a square modulo it. Two successive quadratic extensions of the prime field then give a field of order p^4.
- **claim-graph-fit** (construction, [source](#stmt-op-graph)): The graph $\Gamma$ is triangle-free and has a specified fitting factorization $F_{ij}=\sum_{T\in\mathcal T}\alpha_i(T)\beta_j(T)$ over $\mathbb F_p$ with $t/m<1/100$.
  - Explanation: The threshold-Kneser graph is triangle-free and carries an explicit low-rank fitting factorization whose rank-to-vertex ratio is below 1/100.
- **claim-modular-idempotent** (construction, [source](#stmt-op-modular)): There is a specified idempotent $e\in K[H]$ satisfying, for every $s\in P$, $$(u(s)-1)^\ell e=0,\qquad e(v(s)-1)^\ell=0.$$ Its regular rank $d$ satisfies $d/w>1/96$. Its coefficients are obtained by finite operations in the specified field $K$.
  - Explanation: An explicit idempotent in the local group algebra is killed on the left by a power of one augmentation element and on the right by the same power of the conjugate one. Its regular rank fraction exceeds 1/96, and its coefficients come from finite operations in K.
- **claim-admissible-majority** (bound, [source](#stmt-op-good-count)): More than half of the $\|A_0\|=M^{p^3}$ characters are admissible.
  - Explanation: A strict majority of the characters of the function group have trivial stabilizer and an invertible weighted Gram matrix. This is shown by a union bound together with a grid estimate for nonzero polynomials.
- **claim-edge-vanishing** (theorem, [source](#stmt-op-edge-zero)): For every edge $i<j$ of $\Gamma$, $e_i e_j=0$ in $K[G_0]$.
  - Explanation: After identifying cyclic subgroups along an edge, the incoming and outgoing annihilations force the ordered product of the two idempotents to vanish. The reverse product is not asserted to vanish.
- **claim-triangle-free-gluing** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-triangle-free-gluing)): Let $\Delta$ be a finite simple triangle-free graph. For each vertex $i$, let $D_i$ be a finite group. For each edge $\gamma=\{i,j\}$, let $C_\gamma$ be a nontrivial finite cyclic group, with injective homomorphisms $\iota_{\gamma,i}:C_\gamma\to D_i$ and $\iota_{\gamma,j}:C_\gamma\to D_j$. Assume the following condition at every vertex $i$. If $1\leq k\leq3$, the edges $\gamma_1,\ldots,\gamma_k$ are incident to $i$, and $s_a\in C_{\gamma_a}\setminus\{1\}$, then $$\label{eq:gluing-short-word} \iota_{\gamma_1,i}(s_1)\cdots \iota_{\gamma_k,i}(s_k)\neq1$$ whenever consecutive edge labels are distinct cyclically. For $k=1$ the distinctness condition is omitted; for $k\geq2$ it includes $\gamma_k\neq\gamma_1$. Then every natural homomorphism $D_i\to Q$ is injective, where $$Q=\left(*_{i\in V(\Delta)}D_i\right) \big/\left\langle\!\left\langle \iota_{\gamma,i}(s)\iota_{\gamma,j}(s)^{-1}: \gamma=\{i,j\},\ s\in C_\gamma \right\rangle\!\right\rangle.$$
  - Explanation: Over a triangle-free graph, finite vertex groups glued along cyclic edge groups embed in the quotient provided short alternating words of cyclic-group elements are nontrivial at each vertex. The proof argues by minimal spherical maps and Euler's formula.
- **claim-vertex-groups-embed** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-first-gluing-injective)): Every natural map $H_i\to G_0$ is injective. In particular, the maps $K[H_i]\to K[G_0]$ induced on group algebras are injective.
  - Explanation: The local groups used in the construction satisfy the short-word condition because of a Vandermonde-type independence of the glued vectors and the trivial intersection of U and V. So each vertex group embeds in the glued group.
- **claim-common-sector-rectangular** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-sector-ranks)): Under these assumptions, there is a group $G_1$ containing $G_0$ and a central element $z\in G_1$ of order $M$ with the following property. Set $$R=K[G_1],\qquad f=\frac1M\sum_{k=0}^{M-1}\zeta^{-k}z^k,\qquad S=fR,\qquad P_i=fe_i.$$ Then $f$ is a nonzero central idempotent. There are explicitly specified matrices $$J_i\in\operatorname{Mat}_{w\times d}(S),\qquad Y_i\in\operatorname{Mat}_{d\times w}(S)$$ satisfying $$\label{eq:sector-rectangular} Y_iJ_i=fI_d,\qquad J_iY_i=P_iI_w.$$ The entries of $J_i$ and $Y_i$ lie in the group algebra of a finite subgroup of $G_1$, multiplied by $f$. Their construction uses only finite group operations and linear algebra over $K$.
  - Explanation: A larger group with a central element of order M yields a nonzero central idempotent f. Over the ring fR, each local idempotent admits rectangular matrices that turn its numerical rank d into exact identities.
- **claim-stabilizers-elementary** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-sector-stabilizers)): For each such $\theta$, the subgroup $T_\theta$ is elementary abelian of exponent $p$ and rank at most $3$, with the trivial subgroup allowed.
  - Explanation: Stabilizers of the relevant characters meet the function group trivially. They are therefore subspaces of an elementary abelian group of exponent p and rank at most 3.
- **claim-triangular-compression** (construction, [source](#stmt-op-compression)): Let $S$ be a nonzero unital algebra over a field $K$, and let $m,t,w,d$ be positive integers. Suppose $P_i\in S$ are idempotents and $\alpha_i(T),\beta_j(T)\in K$ satisfy $$F_{ij}=\sum_{T=1}^t\alpha_i(T)\beta_j(T),\qquad F_{ii}=1,\qquad F_{ij}P_iP_j=0\quad(i<j).$$ Suppose there are $J_i\in\operatorname{Mat}_{w\times d}(S)$ and $Y_i\in\operatorname{Mat}_{d\times w}(S)$ with $Y_iJ_i=I_d$, $J_iY_i=P_iI_w$. Then explicit matrices $$C_\sharp\in\operatorname{Mat}_{wt\times md}(S),\qquad D_\sharp\in\operatorname{Mat}_{md\times wt}(S)$$ satisfy $D_\sharp C_\sharp=I_{md}$.
  - Explanation: From a graph factorization, ordered zero products of idempotents and rectangular realizations of their ranks, explicit rectangular matrices with a left inverse are built. The strictly triangular part is nilpotent, so no symmetric orthogonality is needed.
- **claim-square-inverse-certificate** (theorem, [source](#stmt-op-kernel)): Let $R$ be a unital ring, $f\ne0$ a central idempotent, and $S=fR$. Suppose $N>r\ge1$ and matrices $C\in\operatorname{Mat}_{r\times N}(S)$, $D\in\operatorname{Mat}_{N\times r}(S)$ satisfy $DC=fI_N$. Let $\iota:S^r\to S^N$ include the first $r$ coordinates and $\pi:S^N\to S^r$ retain them, with entries $f$ and zero. Then $$A_R=\pi D+(1-f)I_r,\qquad B_R=C\iota+(1-f)I_r$$ satisfy $A_RB_R=I_r$ and $B_RA_R\ne I_r$. More precisely, if $u_*\in S^N$ has last coordinate $f$ and all others zero, then $$v_*:=Cu_*\ne0,\qquad Dv_*=u_*,\qquad A_Rv_*=0,\qquad (B_RA_R-I_r)v_*=-v_*\ne0.$$
  - Explanation: From a split injection into a smaller free module, square matrices with a one-sided inverse are produced, together with an explicit nonzero vector certifying that the reverse product is not the identity.
- **claim-heisenberg-matrix-units** (construction, [source](#stmt-op-heisenberg)): Let $K$ be a field, let $M\ge2$ be invertible in $K$, and let $\zeta\in K$ have order $M$. For an integer $q\ge0$ put $Z_q=(\mathbb Z/M\mathbb Z)^q$ and $n=M^q$. There is a finite group $\Lambda$ of order $Mn^2$ and a nonzero central idempotent $f'\in K[\Lambda]$ for which explicitly specified elements $\Omega_{uv}$, $u,v\in Z_q$, form a basis of $f'K[\Lambda]$ and obey $$\Omega_{uv}\Omega_{st}=\delta_{vs}\Omega_{ut},\qquad \sum_u\Omega_{uu}=f'.$$ Thus $\operatorname{Mat}_n(K)\cong f'K[\Lambda]$ by its standard matrix units.
  - Explanation: A finite Heisenberg-type group has a nonzero central idempotent whose summand of the group algebra is a full matrix algebra with explicit matrix units, even when M is composite.
- **claim-identity-completion** (theorem, [source](#stmt-op-complement)): Let $\Phi:\mathcal A\to\mathcal B$ be an injective ring homomorphism between unital rings, not necessarily preserving their identities, and put $g=\Phi(1_{\mathcal A})$. If $AB=1_{\mathcal A}$ and $BA\ne1_{\mathcal A}$, then $$a=1_{\mathcal B}-g+\Phi(A),\qquad b=1_{\mathcal B}-g+\Phi(B)$$ satisfy $ab=1_{\mathcal B}$ and $ba\ne1_{\mathcal B}$.
  - Explanation: An injective ring homomorphism that need not preserve identities carries a one-sided inverse pair to a pair in the target ring that is still one-sided, once the complement of the image of the identity is added.
- **claim-scalar-transfer** (theorem, [source](#stmt-op-scalar)): Under the field hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#op:heisenberg" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:heisenberg">13</a>, suppose $A,B\in\operatorname{Mat}_r(K[L])$ for a group $L$ and an integer $r\ge1$, with $AB=I_r$ and $BA\ne I_r$. Finite formulas give a finite group $\Lambda$ and $a,b\in K[L\times\Lambda]$ with $ab=1$ and $ba\ne1$. In particular, over any fixed finite field with at least three elements, a failure of stable finiteness transfers to a scalar failure after a finite direct factor, without changing the field.
  - Explanation: A matrix failure of direct finiteness over a group algebra transfers to a scalar failure in the group algebra of a direct product with a finite group, without changing the field.
- **claim-cellular-automaton** (theorem, [source](#stmt-op-cellular)): Let $k$ be a finite field, $L$ a group, and $a,b\in k[L]$ with $ab=1$. Then $T_b:k^L\to k^L$ is an injective cellular automaton, with $$\operatorname{im}T_b=\{y\in k^L:T_{ba}y=y\}.$$ For every $h$ with $(ba-1)_h\ne0$, the configuration $\delta_h$ is absent from its image.
  - Explanation: A one-sided inverse pair over a finite field gives an injective linear cellular automaton whose image is the fixed set of the reverse product. Every point indicator at a support element of the reverse defect is missing from the image.

        "exact_quote": "Let $\\Delta$ be a finite simple triangle-free graph. For each vertex $i$, let $D_i$ be a finite group. If $1\\leq k\\leq3$, the edges $\\gamma_1,\\ldots,\\gamma_k$ are incident to $i$, and $s_a\\in C_{\\gamma_a}\\setminus\\{1\\}$, then $$\\label{eq:gluing-short-word}\n \\iota_{\\gamma_1,i}(s_1)\\cdots\n \\iota_{\\gamma_k,i}(s_k)\\neq1$$ whenever consecutive edge labels are distinct cyclically. <a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **group algebra** ([source](#op:introduction)): A group algebra $K[G]$ consists of finite sums $\sum_g c_g g$, with $c_g\in K$, multiplied by the group law and bilinearity.
- **directly finite and stably finite ring** ([source](#op:introduction)): A unital ring is *directly finite* when $ab=1$ implies $ba=1$; it is *stably finite* when this implication holds in every finite square matrix ring.
- **fitting matrix** ([source](#pm-h-4)): A matrix $F$ *fits* a simple graph when $F_{ii}=1$ and $F_{ij}=0$ for distinct nonadjacent vertices.
- **threshold-Kneser graph adjacency** ([source](#op:finite-section)): Join distinct $i,j$ in $\Gamma$ exactly when $\|I_i\cap I_j\|<200$.
- **regular rank** ([source](#op:modular-section)): The *regular rank* of $e\in K[H]$ means $\dim_K eK[H]$, the dimension of the image of left multiplication.
- **admissible character** ([source](#pm-h-10)): Call $\lambda$ admissible if its stabilizer in $P$ is trivial and $N_\lambda$ is invertible.
- **exceptional spherical map** ([source](#pm-h-15)): Call the map *exceptional* if precisely one face has nonidentity evaluation and all other faces have identity evaluation.
- **ordinary face** ([source](#pm-h-15)): A face is *ordinary* when this evaluation is the identity.
- **cellular automaton** ([source](#pm-h-6)): A cellular automaton has one finite memory set $E\subset G$ and one rule applied to $(x(gu))_{u\in E}$ at every $g$.
- **surjunctive group** ([source](#pm-h-6)): A group is *surjunctive* when every injective such automaton, over every finite alphabet, is surjective.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-field-tower** [claim](#stmt-op-field) / [proof](#proof-stmt-op-field): Show the least divisor is a prime exceeding m, extract a square root of -1, and adjoin two successive square roots of nonsquares. -> field K of order p^4
- **step-graph-fit** [claim](#stmt-op-graph) / [proof](#proof-stmt-op-graph): Rule out triangles by a union-size count and build the fitting factorization from a multilinearized threshold polynomial, then bound the rank ratio. -> triangle-free graph with low-rank fit
- **step-good-count** [claim](#stmt-op-good-count) / [proof](#proof-stmt-op-good-count): Union bound on stabilizers plus a Cauchy-Binet expansion and a grid bound for nonzero polynomials. -> more than half of the characters are admissible
- **step-modular-idempotent** [claim](#stmt-op-modular) / [proof](#op:explicit-e): Assemble the idempotent blockwise from Fourier orbit units and weighted projections, then count the regular rank and check both annihilations. -> idempotent e with rank fraction above 1/96
- **step-edge-zero** [claim](#stmt-op-edge-zero) / [proof](#proof-stmt-op-edge-zero): In the local cyclic algebra, convert the outgoing annihilation into a divisibility statement and multiply by the incoming annihilation. -> ordered product e_i e_j equals zero
- **step-gluing-criterion** [claim](#stmt-lem-triangle-free-gluing) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-triangle-free-gluing): Encode a failed injection as an exceptional spherical map, reduce a minimal one, and contradict Euler's formula. -> injectivity of vertex groups in the glued quotient
- **step-gluing-application** [claim](#stmt-prop-first-gluing-injective) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-first-gluing-injective): Check the short-word hypothesis using a Vandermonde-type determinant and the trivial intersection of U and V. -> each H_i embeds in G_0
- **step-stabilizers** [claim](#stmt-lem-sector-stabilizers) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-sector-stabilizers): Show the stabilizer misses the function group by a cycle-length argument, so it embeds in the additive group of F_p^3. -> elementary abelian stabilizers of rank at most 3
- **step-common-sector** [claim](#stmt-prop-sector-ranks) / [proof](#pm-h-22): Enlarge the group with a central element, decompose the central sector into matrix blocks, and use nilpotent augmentation ideals to build rectangular matrices. -> matrices J_i and Y_i with exact rectangular identities
- **step-compression** [claim](#stmt-op-compression) / [proof](#proof-stmt-op-compression): Form the triangular product, invert it using a finite nilpotent series, and compose with block-diagonal and rectangular realizations. -> rectangular pair with left inverse
- **step-kernel** [claim](#stmt-op-kernel) / [proof](#proof-stmt-op-kernel): Complete the rectangular pair to square matrices and exhibit an explicit kernel vector. -> square one-sided inverse with nonzero defect
- **step-heisenberg** [claim](#stmt-op-heisenberg) / [proof](#proof-stmt-op-heisenberg): Define the group law, a central idempotent and shift-modulation matrix units, and prove independence through an explicit representation. -> matrix algebra inside a finite group algebra
- **step-complement** [claim](#stmt-op-complement) / [proof](#proof-stmt-op-complement): Add the complement of the image of the identity and expand both products. -> unital pair with one-sided product
- **step-scalar** [claim](#stmt-op-scalar) / [proof](#proof-stmt-op-scalar): Pad matrices to size M^q, embed through matrix units, and apply the identity completion. -> scalar elements a and b
- **step-cellular** [claim](#stmt-op-cellular) / [proof](#proof-stmt-op-cellular): Use multiplicativity of the automaton map to get injectivity, identify the image as fixed points, and test a point indicator. -> injective automaton missing point indicators
- **step-main-assembly** [claim](#stmt-op-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-op-scalar-2): Chain the field, graph, idempotent, gluing, central sector, compression, certificate and scalar transfer, then check finite generation and torsion. -> counterexample over the field of order p^4

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-heisenberg-matrix-units**: For a field containing an invertible integer M at least two and an element of exact order M, the explicit shift-and-modulation elements of the finite Heisenberg group multiply as matrix units inside the central sector of its group algebra. Their products vanish unless the inner indices match, and the diagonal ones sum to the sector idempotent.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-finite-group-counterexample**: Reading the finite matrix-unit check as evidence that a finite group algebra can have a one-sided inverse that is not two-sided. -> The check only certifies a full matrix algebra inside a finite group algebra. Finite group algebras are finite-dimensional, so a one-sided inverse there is always two-sided.
- **anti-noninvertible-order**: Running the encoding over a field in which the cyclic order M is not invertible. -> The idempotent and the Fourier-type projections need division by M and by n, which fails when the characteristic divides M.
- **anti-practical-evaluation**: Treating the toy run as an evaluation of the full counterexample over the displayed prime. -> The harness runs tiny parameters only; the real field and group are astronomically larger and no such computation is claimed.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The Heisenberg-block proposition is an explicit finite construction: group law, central idempotent and matrix units are given by finite formulas, so their identities can be checked exactly on small parameters.

```python
# INPUTS: one line 'p M zeta q' with p prime, M>=2 invertible mod p, zeta of exact order M mod p, q>=0.
# PRECONDITIONS: p prime, gcd(M,p)=1, zeta has multiplicative order exactly M modulo p; parameters are small.
# OUTPUTS: one line 'n=.. order=.. units_ok=0/1 trace_ok=0/1 rank=..' for the Heisenberg block.
# TIME: polynomial in the number of group elements; about n^4 products of sparse group-algebra elements.
# SPACE: sparse dictionaries over the group of order M*n*n.
# ARITHMETIC: exact integer arithmetic modulo p.
# FAILURE: invalid parameters are outside the contract; an exception gives a nonzero exit.
import sys, itertools
data = sys.stdin.read().split()
p, M, z, q = [int(t) for t in data]
n = M ** q
zi = pow(z, -1, p)
inv_n = pow(n, -1, p)
invM = pow(M, -1, p)
pts = list(itertools.product(range(M), repeat=q))
z0 = tuple([0] * q)

def dot(a, b):
    return sum(i * j for i, j in zip(a, b)) % M

def add(a, b):
    return tuple((i + j) % M for i, j in zip(a, b))

def sub(a, b):
    return tuple((i - j) % M for i, j in zip(a, b))

def gmul(g, h):
    x, y, k = g
    x2, y2, k2 = h
    return (add(x, x2), add(y, y2), (k + k2 + dot(y, x2)) % M)

def amul(A, B):
    out = {}
    for g, c in A.items():
        for h, d in B.items():
            r = gmul(g, h)
            out[r] = (out.get(r, 0) + c * d) % p
    return {g: c for g, c in out.items() if c}

fp = {(z0, z0, k): pow(zi, k, p) * invM % p for k in range(M)}

def Dv(v):
    tot = {}
    for y in pts:
        c = pow(zi, dot(y, v), p) * inv_n % p
        term = amul(fp, {(z0, y, 0): 1})
        for g, d in term.items():
            tot[g] = (tot.get(g, 0) + c * d) % p
    return {g: c for g, c in tot.items() if c}

Ds = {v: Dv(v) for v in pts}
Om = {}
for u in pts:
    for v in pts:
        Xe = {(sub(u, v), z0, 0): 1}
        Om[(u, v)] = amul(Xe, Ds[v])

units_ok = 1
for u in pts:
    for v in pts:
        for s in pts:
            for t in pts:
                lhs = amul(Om[(u, v)], Om[(s, t)])
                rhs = Om[(u, t)] if v == s else {}
                if lhs != rhs:
                    units_ok = 0

tot = {}
for u in pts:
    for g, c in Om[(u, u)].items():
        tot[g] = (tot.get(g, 0) + c) % p
tot = {g: c for g, c in tot.items() if c}
trace_ok = 1 if tot == fp else 0

idx = {}
basis = {}
rank = 0
for u in pts:
    for v in pts:
        r = {}
        for g, c in Om[(u, v)].items():
            if g not in idx:
                idx[g] = len(idx)
            r[idx[g]] = c
        while r:
            piv = min(r)
            if piv in basis:
                c = r[piv]
                b = basis[piv]
                for key, val in b.items():
                    r[key] = (r.get(key, 0) - c * val) % p
                r = {k: x for k, x in r.items() if x}
            else:
                iv = pow(r[piv], -1, p)
                basis[piv] = {k: x * iv % p for k, x in r.items()}
                rank += 1
                break

print("n=%d order=%d units_ok=%d trace_ok=%d rank=%d" % (n, M * n * n, units_ok, trace_ok, rank))
```

j) % M for i, j in zip(a, b))\n\ndef gmul(g, h):\n    x, y, k = g\n    x2, y2, k2 = h\n    return (add(x, x2), add(y, y2), (k + k2 + dot(y, x2)) % M)\n\ndef amul(A, B):\n    out = {}\n    for g, c in A.items():\n        for h, d in B.items():\n            r = gmul(g, h)\n            out[r] = (out.get(r, 0) + c * d) % p\n    return {g: c for g, c in out.items() if c}\n\nfp = {(z0, z0, k): pow(zi, k, p) * invM % p for k in range(M)}\n\ndef Dv(v):\n    tot = {}\n    for y in pts:\n        c = pow(zi, dot(y, v), p) * inv_n % p\n        term = amul(fp, {(z0, y, 0): 1})\n        for g, d in term.items():\n            tot[g] = (tot.get(g, 0) + c * d) % p\n    return {g: c for g, c in tot.items() if c}\n\nDs = {v: Dv(v) for v in pts}\nOm = {}\nfor u in pts:\n    for v in pts:\n        Xe = {(sub(u, v), z0, 0): 1}\n        Om[(u, v)] = amul(Xe, Ds[v])\n\nunits_ok = 1\nfor u in pts:\n    for v in pts:\n        for s in pts:\n            for t in pts:\n                lhs = amul(Om[(u, v)], Om[(s, t)])\n                rhs = Om[(u, t)] if v == s else {}\n                if lhs != rhs:\n                    units_ok = 0\n\ntot = {}\nfor u in pts:\n    for g, c in Om[(u, u)].items():\n        tot[g] = (tot.get(g, 0) + c) % p\ntot = {g: c for g, c in tot.items() if c}\ntrace_ok = 1 if tot == fp else 0\n\nidx = {}\nbasis = {}\nrank = 0\nfor u in pts:\n    for v in pts:\n        r = {}\n        for g, c in Om[(u, v)].items():\n            if g not in idx:\n                idx[g] = len(idx)\n            r[idx[g]] = c\n        while r:\n            piv = min(r)\n            if piv in basis:\n                c = r[piv]\n                b = basis[piv]\n                for key, val in b.items():\n                    r[key] = (r.get(key, 0) - c * val) % p\n                r = {k: x for k, x in r.items() if x}\n            else:\n                iv = pow(r[piv], -1, p)\n                basis[piv] = {k: x * iv % p for k, x in r.items()}\n                rank += 1\n                break\n\nprint(\"n=%d order=%d units_ok=%d trace_ok=%d rank=%d\" % (n, M * n * n, units_ok, trace_ok, rank))\n",
    "test_vectors": [
      {
        "expected": "n=4 order=64 units_ok=1 trace_ok=1 rank=16",
        "input": "5 4 2 1"
      },
      {
        "expected": "n=4 order=32 units_ok=1 trace_ok=1 rank=16",
        "input": "3 2 2 2"
      },
      {
        "expected": "n=3 order=27 units_ok=1 trace_ok=1 rank=9",
        "input": "7 3 2 1"
      },
      {
        "expected": "n=1 order=4 units_ok=1 trace_ok=1 rank=1",
        "input": "5 4 2 0"
      },
      {
        "expected": "n=4 order=32 units_ok=1 trace_ok=1 rank=16",
        "input": "5 2 4 2"
      },
      {
        "expected": "n=2 order=8 units_ok=1 trace_ok=1 rank=4",
        "input": "7 2 6 1"
      }
    ]
  },
  "pm_id": "PM-197.4",

}

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result is an existence theorem for specific algebraic objects. The group's word problem is never solved, and the paper does not claim the construction has been evaluated as a practical computation. A library would have no executable multiplication for the group. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No dynamical process is simulated. The only dynamics is a linear cellular automaton on all configurations of an infinite group. Its nonsurjectivity is witnessed by a point indicator that has no preimage. This is a counterexample to a conjecture, not a stepping model. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper supplies no checker, solver or verifier interface for users. A one-sided-inverse test inside a group algebra of this group is not offered, and the kernel vector is only a proof certificate. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper contains finite explicit prescriptions, but they are steps of an existence proof. No complexity is stated, and the prime p is defined through a huge factorial with no stated way to compute it. Panel-3-style execution is therefore not sound. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No representation or index structure is defined. The group is a quotient of free products by relations, and equality of its elements is not decided by any structure in the paper. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The data are non-computed algebraic objects: a field of order p^4 for a huge odd prime p, with a group algebra over a huge finite group. No arithmetic circuit or kernel is described, and nothing suggests a hardware mapping. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper makes no hardness assumption and gives no protocol. The only finite field is the specific one of order p^4. The theorem is about one-sided inverses in a group algebra and carries no security guarantee. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No learning, data distribution or model is present. The weighted pairing argument counts admissible characters and is not a statistical estimate. The result is purely algebraic. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No objective function or feasible set is optimised. The comparison of a triangle-free graph's fitting rank ratio with the idempotent rank ratio is a counting argument for a contradiction. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There are no nodes, messages or consistency properties. Cayley-graph networks over this group are not treated, and no protocol uses the one-sided inverse. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The natural home is pure algebra and dynamics: a negative answer to a conjecture on direct finiteness, with a consequence for surjunctivity. This is not a transferable engineering contract, and the statements are purely existential constructions. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-single-prime** (hypothesis): The negative answer is established for the one displayed odd prime and its field of order p^4. It says nothing about any other odd characteristic. Blocked use: ['Citing the result as a counterexample in every odd characteristic', 'Replacing the displayed prime by another prime without redoing the construction']
- **limit-no-practical-evaluation** (nonconstructive): The finite prescriptions are not claimed to have been run. The prime is defined through a huge factorial, and the group is a quotient whose word problem is never solved. The result is an existence statement, not a working computation. Blocked use: ['Implementing multiplication in K[G] as a library routine', 'Quoting a running time for producing a and b']
- **limit-infinite-group-needed** (edge_case): Direct finiteness always holds for finite groups, including in modular characteristic. The counterexample therefore lives only in an infinite group, and finite tests of local ingredients cannot exhibit the failure. Blocked use: ['Searching finite group algebras for a one-sided inverse that is not two-sided', 'Reading a finite matrix-unit check as evidence of the failure']
- **limit-nonzero-field-order-invertible** (hypothesis): The matrix-unit encoding in a finite Heisenberg-type group needs M invertible in the field and an element of exact order M. The Fourier-type idempotents divide by M and by n. Blocked use: ['Running the encoding where the characteristic divides M', 'Using a field lacking an element of order M']
- **limit-field-quadratic-tower** (hypothesis): The field of order p^4 is obtained as a two-step quadratic tower, which depends on choosing a nonsquare in the prime field. A different choice of tower data is outside the stated lemma. Blocked use: ['Applying the lemma with a square in place of a nonsquare', 'Assuming the tower is a field for a prime not satisfying the stated conditions']
- **limit-gluing-triangle-free** (hypothesis): The injectivity criterion for the glued group requires a finite simple triangle-free graph and finite vertex groups. Graphs with triangles are not covered by it. Blocked use: ['Gluing along a graph containing a triangle and still assuming vertex groups embed', 'Applying the criterion to infinite vertex groups']
- **limit-ordered-zero-product** (edge_case): Only the ordered products of idempotents along edges are shown to vanish. The reverse product is not asserted to vanish, and the compression step is built to need only the ordered one. Blocked use: ['Assuming the idempotents are mutually orthogonal', 'Deducing a symmetric vanishing for the reverse product']
- **limit-strict-dimension-drop** (hypothesis): The square one-sided inverse and its kernel certificate need the strict inequality N greater than r. Without a strict dimension drop there is no nonzero certificate vector. Blocked use: ['Applying the certificate lemma when the compressed size equals the original size', 'Dropping the strict rank comparison between the two ratios']
- **limit-cellular-finite-alphabet** (formal_scope): The automaton transfer is stated over a finite field and for elements with ab equal to 1. It gives an injective automaton whose image is a fixed-point set and misses point indicators at the support of the reverse defect. Blocked use: ['Applying it to infinite coefficient rings as a finite-alphabet automaton', 'Applying it to a pair without ab equal to 1']
- **limit-graph-triangle-free-only** (approximation): The rank-to-vertex ratio bound is for the specific threshold-Kneser instance, which is triangle-free. Other graphs need their own factorization and ratio estimate. Blocked use: ['Reusing the ratio bound for an arbitrary triangle-free graph', 'Reading the graph result as a general storage-code statement']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 197 (`lean/docs/197.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [A Counterexample to Kaplansky's Direct-Finiteness Conjecture in Characteristic Two](../../preprints/A-Counterexample-to-Kaplanskys-Direct-Finiteness-Conjecture-in-Characteristic-Two-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [A Counterexample to the Group-Ring Determinant Conjecture](../../preprints/A-Counterexample-to-the-Group-Ring-Determinant-Conjecture-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [A Counterexample to Kaplansky's Direct-Finiteness Conjecture in Odd Characteristic](../../preprints/A-Counterexample-to-Kaplanskys-Direct-Finiteness-Conjecture-in-Odd-Characteristic-September-26-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> Kaplansky's direct-finiteness conjecture asserts that $ab=1$ implies $ba=1$ for $a,b\in K[G]$, for every field $K$ and group $G$. The formalized results construct a finite field of characteristic two and a group algebra violating this implication. One statement records the counterexample for a finitely generated group; the more detailed construction gives a finitely presented group with an element of odd prime order.
>
> The detailed construction also gives a precise recipe for choosing the data and proves that the required search terminates. The further conclusion that the group is nonsofic is outside these statements.
>
> A nonsingular integer matrix has absolute determinant at least $1$. The group-ring Determinant Conjecture extends this bound to matrices over $\mathbb Z[G]$ for every discrete group $G$. If $T_A$ is the operator induced by such a matrix $A$ on finite direct sums of $\ell^2(G)$, and $\mu_A$ is the spectral measure of $T_A^*T_A$ with respect to the group trace, the conjecture asserts
>
> $\displaystyle \int_{(0,\infty)}\log t\,d\mu_A(t)\ge0,$
>
> with the zero spectral atom omitted. For an invertible square matrix, this is equivalent to its Fuglede–Kadison determinant being at least $1$.
>
> The formalized result contradicts that bound. It gives a finitely generated group $G$ and an $n\times n$ matrix over $\mathbb Z[G]$, with $n\ge1$, that is invertible over $\mathbb Q[G]$. Its bounded left-regular operator is invertible and has determinant strictly between $0$ and $1$.
>
> The formalization uses the trace of $\log(T^*T)$ to define the determinant for the invertible operator $T$. The paper's additional spectral-measure integral conclusion is not included.
>
> Kaplansky's direct-finiteness conjecture asserts that $ab=1$ implies $ba=1$ in every group algebra over a field. Let $p$ be the smallest prime divisor of $\bigl(\binom{1200}{600}!\bigr)^2+1$; this prime is odd. The formalized result constructs a field $K$ of order $p^4$, a finitely generated group $G$ with torsion, and elements $a,b$ violating this implication. The associated cellular automaton on all configurations $K^G$ is injective but not surjective.
>
> The formalization also contains fixed-field transfer results, separate from the statement linked below.

| declaration | kind |
|---|---|
| `MainClaim` | def |
| `main_theorem` | theorem |

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace KaplanskyCounterexample

def MainClaim : Prop :=
  ∃ (K : Type) (_ : Field K) (_ : Fintype K) (_ : CharP K 2),
    ∃ (G : Type) (_ : Group G) (_ : Group.FG G),
      ∃ a b : MonoidAlgebra K G, a * b = 1 ∧ b * a ≠ 1

theorem main_theorem : MainClaim := by
  sorry

end KaplanskyCounterexample

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We construct a counterexample to Kaplansky's direct-finiteness conjecture in odd characteristic. For one specified odd prime <i>p</i>, we obtain a field <i>K</i> of order <i>p</i><sup>4</sup>, a finitely generated group <i>G</i> containing torsion, and finite sums $`a,b\in K[G]`$ with $`ab=1`$ but $`ba\ne1`$. The same elements define a cellular automaton on <i>K</i><sup><i>G</i></sup> that is injective but not surjective.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>
#### Statements index

| anchor | kind | label | number | first sentence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| [stmt-op-main](#stmt-op-main) | theorem | `op:main` | 1 | Put |
| [stmt-op-field](#stmt-op-field) | lemma | `op:field` | 2 | For $m,p$ in Theorem 1, $p>m$ is an odd prime and $-1$ is a square in $\mathbb F_p$. |
| [stmt-op-graph](#stmt-op-graph) | proposition | `op:graph` | 3 | The graph $\Gamma$ is triangle-free and has a specified fitting factorization $F_{ij}=\sum_{T\in\mathcal T}\alpha_i(T)\beta_j(T)$ over $\mathbb F_p$ with $t/m<1/100$.* |
| [stmt-op-modular](#stmt-op-modular) | proposition | `op:modular` | 4 | There is a specified idempotent $e\in K[H]$ satisfying, for every $s\in P$, $$(u(s)-1)^\ell e=0,\qquad e(v(s)-1)^\ell=0.$$ Its regular rank $d$ satisfies $d/w>1/96$. |
| [stmt-op-good-count](#stmt-op-good-count) | lemma | `op:good-count` | 5 | More than half of the $&#124;A_0&#124;=M^{p^3}$ characters are admissible.* |
| [stmt-op-edge-zero](#stmt-op-edge-zero) | corollary | `op:edge-zero` | 6 | For every edge |
| [stmt-lem-triangle-free-gluing](#stmt-lem-triangle-free-gluing) | lemma | `lem:triangle-free-gluing` | 7 | Let $\Delta$ be a finite simple triangle-free graph. |
| [stmt-prop-first-gluing-injective](#stmt-prop-first-gluing-injective) | proposition | `prop:first-gluing-injective` | 8 | Every natural map $H_i\to G_0$ is injective. |
| [stmt-prop-sector-ranks](#stmt-prop-sector-ranks) | proposition | `prop:sector-ranks` | 9 | Under these assumptions, there is a group $G_1$ containing $G_0$ and a central element $z\in G_1$ of order $M$ with the following property. |
| [stmt-lem-sector-stabilizers](#stmt-lem-sector-stabilizers) | lemma | `lem:sector-stabilizers` | 10 | For each such $\theta$, the subgroup $T_\theta$ is elementary abelian of exponent $p$ and rank at most $3$, with the trivial subgroup allowed.* |
| [stmt-op-compression](#stmt-op-compression) | lemma | `op:compression` | 11 | Let $S$ be a nonzero unital algebra over a field $K$, and let $m,t,w,d$ be positive integers. |
| [stmt-op-kernel](#stmt-op-kernel) | lemma | `op:kernel` | 12 | Let $R$ be a unital ring, $f\ne0$ a central idempotent, and $S=fR$. |
| [stmt-op-heisenberg](#stmt-op-heisenberg) | proposition | `op:heisenberg` | 13 | Let $K$ be a field, let $M\ge2$ be invertible in $K$, and let $\zeta\in K$ have order $M$. |
| [stmt-op-complement](#stmt-op-complement) | lemma | `op:complement` | 14 | Let $\Phi:\mathcal A\to\mathcal B$ be an injective ring homomorphism between unital rings, not necessarily preserving their identities, and put $g=\Phi(1_{\mathcal A})$. |
| [stmt-op-scalar](#stmt-op-scalar) | corollary | `op:scalar` | 15 | Under the field hypotheses of Proposition 13, suppose $A,B\in\operatorname{Mat}_r(K[L])$ for a group $L$ and an integer $r\ge1$, with $AB=I_r$ and $BA\ne I_r$. |
| [stmt-op-cellular](#stmt-op-cellular) | proposition | `op:cellular` | 16 | Let $k$ be a finite field, $L$ a group, and $a,b\in k[L]$ with $ab=1$. |

<a id="op:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

A group algebra $K[G]$ consists of finite sums $\sum_g c_g g$, with $c_g\in K$, multiplied by the group law and bilinearity. A unital ring is *directly finite* when $ab=1$ implies $ba=1$; it is *stably finite* when this implication holds in every finite square matrix ring. Kaplansky’s direct-finiteness conjecture asks whether $K[G]$ is directly finite for every field $K$ and every group $G$, including groups with torsion. The result here is a negative answer in one specified odd characteristic.

<a id="stmt-op-main"></a>
<div id="op:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *Put $$m=\binom{1200}{600},\qquad
 p=\min\{h\in\mathbb Z:h>1,\ h\mid(m!)^2+1\}.$$ Then $p$ is an odd prime. There are a specified field $K$ of order $p^4$, a specified finitely generated group $G$ containing torsion, and specified finite sums $a,b\in K[G]$ satisfying $ab=1$ and $ba\ne1$.*

</div>

The word “specified” refers to finite formulas and choices in ordered finite sets. The field is a quadratic tower, the group is built from finitely many finite groups and explicit identifications, and the sums are defined without solving the word problem of that group. The construction is not claimed to have been evaluated as a practical computation. It concerns the displayed prime, not every odd prime.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
###### The finiteness problem.

For finite $G$, the algebra and all its finite matrix rings are finite-dimensional over $K$. A one-sided inverse between their left multiplication operators is therefore two-sided. This argument works even when the characteristic divides $|G|$. The difficulty for an infinite group is to obtain a corresponding conclusion from finite support alone.

Kaplansky proved stable finiteness in characteristic zero using finite von Neumann algebras, and asked whether the one-sided-inverse property persists in positive characteristic (Kaplansky 1972, 122–23) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Kaplansky1972](#ref-Kaplansky1972)]. Ara, O’Meara, and Perera proved stable finiteness for free-by-amenable groups over arbitrary division rings (Ara, O’Meara, and Perera 2002) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[AraOMearaPerera2002](#ref-AraOMearaPerera2002)]. Elek and Szabó (Elek and Szabó 2004, Corollary 4.7) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[ElekSzabo2004](#ref-ElekSzabo2004)] established the same conclusion for all sofic groups. Such groups admit finite permutation models that approximate multiplication while keeping distinct elements separated. These theorems are constraints on the example, not hypotheses about its group. In particular, Elek–Szabó’s theorem implies, after the scalar construction is completed, that $G$ cannot be sofic.

A separate characteristic-two construction uses incidence structures, polynomial pairings, a planar group-embedding argument, and character packing (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[OpenAICharacteristicTwo2026](#ref-OpenAICharacteristicTwo2026)]. The construction here has a different local mechanism. A high-degree subspace of a modular translation representation is annihilated on one side; a weighted pairing gives a compatible annihilation on the other. The characteristic-two theorem is not an input to this odd-prime argument, and the two methods retain distinct coefficient hypotheses.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
###### The two quantities that must be compared.

A matrix $F$ *fits* a simple graph when $F_{ii}=1$ and $F_{ij}=0$ for distinct nonadjacent vertices. We use a triangle-free graph on $m$ vertices and a specified factorization $$F_{ij}=\sum_{T\in\mathcal T}\alpha_i(T)\beta_j(T),\qquad
 |\mathcal T|=t,\qquad \frac{t}{m}<\frac1{100}.$$ This is an explicit threshold-Kneser instance of the polynomial constructions of Haviv and Golovnev–Haviv (Haviv 2018, Proposition 13) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Haviv2018Theta](#ref-Haviv2018Theta)] <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[GolovnevHaviv2022](#ref-GolovnevHaviv2022)] (Golovnev and Haviv 2022, Theorem 3.2 and Lemma 3.5), whose polynomial ancestry includes Alon–Babai–Suzuki (Alon, Babai, and Suzuki 1991) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[AlonBabaiSuzuki1991](#ref-AlonBabaiSuzuki1991)]. Low-rank fitting matrices also give storage-code recovery equations. The triangle-free constructions of Barg–Zémor, Huang–Xiang, and Barg–Schwartz–Yohananov provide related context (Barg and Zémor 2022; Huang and Xiang 2023; Barg, Schwartz, and Yohananov 2024) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[BargZemor2022](#ref-BargZemor2022), [HuangXiang2023](#ref-HuangXiang2023), [BargSchwartzYohananov2024](#ref-BargSchwartzYohananov2024)]. Their results are not used to supply a group-algebra idempotent.

At every vertex we use a copy of a finite group $H$ and an idempotent $e\in K[H]$. Write $w=|H|$ and $d=\dim_K eK[H]$, the rank of left multiplication by $e$ on the regular representation. The second quantity is $$\frac d w>\frac1{96}.$$ The idempotents satisfy a directed condition: after identifying certain cyclic subgroups at adjacent vertices, $e_i e_j=0$ for every edge with $i<j$. There is no assertion about the reverse product. The asymmetry is what allows a triangular calculation to combine the two strict bounds.

The characteristic-dependent construction appears in Section <a href="#op:modular-section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:modular-section">3</a>. High augmentation degree gives the annihilated columns, in the elementary abelian setting of Jennings’ theory (Jennings 1941) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Jennings1941](#ref-Jennings1941)]. A diagonal weighted Gram determinant and Schwartz’s grid bound (Schwartz 1980, Lemma 1 and Corollary 1) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Schwartz1980](#ref-Schwartz1980)], with related polynomial-identity work of Zippel (Zippel 1979) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Zippel1979](#ref-Zippel1979)], supply enough nondegenerate pairings. Fourier character orbits turn these projections into actual elements of $K[H]$. The full algebra need not be semisimple; only its prime-to-$p$ normal abelian subgroup is diagonalized, in the character-orbit setting of Clifford (Clifford 1937) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Clifford1937Announcement](#ref-Clifford1937Announcement)].

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### From the comparison to scalar elements.

The proof has three transitions that numerical rank inequalities alone cannot justify. First, the quotient identifying the cyclic subgroups must retain the finite vertex groups. Its injectivity proof uses the $C(4)$–$T(4)$ graphical-complex mechanism described by Prytuła (Prytuła 2024, sec. 8) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Prytula2024](#ref-Prytula2024)]: local short-word restrictions and triangle-freeness rule out a spherical map with exactly one nontrivial face. Ordinary graph-of-groups normal form does not by itself establish this first quotient’s injectivity. Section <a href="#sec:gluing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:gluing">4</a> proves it locally, including the reductions for loops and bridges.

Second, regular ranks must become identities over the same ring. A further group enlargement $G_1$ has a nonzero central idempotent $f$ in $R=K[G_1]$. In the ring $S=fR$, whose identity is $f$, the elements $P_i=fe_i$ admit rectangular matrices $$J_i\in\operatorname{Mat}_{w\times d}(S),\qquad
 Y_i\in\operatorname{Mat}_{d\times w}(S),\qquad
 Y_iJ_i=fI_d,\quad J_iY_i=P_iI_w.$$ The finite-group blocks in this step have elementary abelian $p$-group stabilizers, possibly nontrivial and nonnormal. Their group algebras have nilpotent augmentation ideals, and a finite change of basis proves exact integer rank identities before coordinates are paired. Section <a href="#sec:sector" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:sector">5</a> gives the full construction of these matrices, with their entries in explicitly attached finite subgroups.

Third, a matrix counterexample does not yet give two scalar elements of the same group algebra. In Section <a href="#op:compression-section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:compression-section">6</a>, the fit and the ordered zero products produce a split injection $$S^{md}\longrightarrow S^{wt}\longrightarrow S^{md},
 \qquad wt<md,$$ whose composite is the identity. We derive square matrices with a one-sided inverse and certify the reverse defect by a nonzero kernel vector. Section <a href="#op:scalar-section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:scalar-section">7</a> then implements the finite-factor reduction of Dykema–Juschenko (Dykema and Juschenko 2015, Lemma 2.1 and Theorem 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[DykemaJuschenko2015](#ref-DykemaJuschenko2015)]. A finite Heisenberg group gives explicit matrix units, their linear independence makes the encoding injective, and a complementary idempotent restores the full scalar identity. The shift and modulation model is classical (Gurevich and Hadani 2009, sec. 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[GurevichHadani2009](#ref-GurevichHadani2009)]; the composite cyclic order and finite coefficient field used here are checked directly.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### The dynamical consequence.

A configuration over a finite alphabet $A$ is any function $G\to A$. A cellular automaton has one finite memory set $E\subset G$ and one rule applied to $(x(gu))_{u\in E}$ at every $g$. A group is *surjunctive* when every injective such automaton, over every finite alphabet, is surjective. Gottschalk proposed this for all groups (Gottschalk 1973) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Gottschalk1973](#ref-Gottschalk1973)]; Gromov and Weiss proved it for sofic groups (Gromov 1999; Weiss 2000) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Gromov1999](#ref-Gromov1999), [Weiss2000](#ref-Weiss2000)]. The group-ring connection is recorded by Elek–Szabó and developed in the linear theory of Ceccherini-Silberstein–Coornaert (Elek and Szabó 2004; Ceccherini-Silberstein and Coornaert 2006) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[ElekSzabo2004](#ref-ElekSzabo2004), [CSCCoornaert2006](#ref-CSCCoornaert2006)]. Phung proved stable finiteness over every field for surjunctive groups (Phung 2023, Theorem B) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Phung2023](#ref-Phung2023)]; subsequent proofs and extensions include (Ceccherini-Silberstein, Coornaert, and Phung 2025a, 2025b) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[CSCPhungGroup2025](#ref-CSCPhungGroup2025), [CSCPhungMonoid2025](#ref-CSCPhungMonoid2025)].

Section <a href="#op:cellular-section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:cellular-section">8</a> gives the elementary transfer with its actual multiplication convention. The coefficients of $b$ define an injective automaton, those of $a$ give a left inverse, and a nonzero coefficient of $ba-1$ specifies a missing point indicator. The argument excludes preimages of arbitrary support. It is a consequence of the scalar construction, not a further existence theorem.

All matrices below act on columns of free right modules. Matrix entries therefore multiply coordinates on the left. Transpose is ordinary transpose over $K$, with no involution. Cardinalities and dimensions in rank comparisons are integers, not elements of the coefficient field. For a positive integer $n$, write $[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}$.

<a id="op:finite-section"></a>
### The field, the graph, and the local group

We first construct the finite data. This fixes every coefficient field and denominator before the modular argument begins.

<a id="stmt-op-field"></a>
<div id="op:field" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2**. *For $m,p$ in Theorem <a href="#op:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:main">1</a>, $p>m$ is an odd prime and $-1$ is a square in $\mathbb F_p$. If $\eta$ is a nonsquare in $\mathbb F_p^*$, then $$K_0=\mathbb F_p[\xi]/(\xi^2-\eta),\qquad
 K=K_0[\nu]/(\nu^2-\xi)$$ are quadratic field extensions, so $|K|=p^4$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-op-field"><summary>Proof of stmt-op-field</summary>

*Proof.* A least divisor greater than one of an integer is prime: any nontrivial proper divisor of a composite least divisor would be smaller. If $p\le m$, then $p$ divides both $m!$ and $(m!)^2+1$, which is impossible. Hence $p>m>600$, and the defining divisibility gives $(m!)^2=-1$ in $\mathbb F_p$. There are nonsquares because each nonzero square has exactly two square roots. The first quotient is therefore a quadratic field. The conjugate of $\xi$ is $-\xi$, so its norm is $-\eta$. Since $-1$ is square, $-\eta$ is nonsquare. A square in $K_0$ would have square norm, proving that $\xi$ is nonsquare and that the second quotient is a field. ◻

</details>

Choose the least nonsquare using representatives $0,\ldots,p-1$; order $K$ by its coefficients in $(1,\xi,\nu,\xi\nu)$. Put $M=p^4-1$ and choose the least element $\zeta\in K^*$ of order $M$. This element exists: if $b$ is the least common multiple of all orders in $K^*$, then $b\mid M$, while the root bound for $X^b-1$ gives $M\le b$. For each prime-power factor of $b$, an appropriate power of one element has that exact order. Multiplying the resulting commuting elements of coprime orders gives order $b=M$. In particular, $M$ and all its powers are invertible in $K$.

Use natural orders on integer indices, increasing-list lexicographic orders on subsets, and lexicographic orders on tuples and finite function tables. Order characters of $(\mathbb Z/M\mathbb Z)^D$ by their exponent lists on the ordered coordinate generators. These conventions specify the finite choices below.

Let $I_1,\ldots,I_m$ be the $600$-subsets of $[1200]$ in order. Join distinct $i,j$ in $\Gamma$ exactly when $|I_i\cap I_j|<200$. Put $$\mathcal T=\{T\subseteq[1200]:|T|\le400\},\qquad
 t=\sum_{j=0}^{400}\binom{1200}{j}.$$

<a id="stmt-op-graph"></a>
<div id="op:graph" class="proposition">

**Proposition 3**. *The graph $\Gamma$ is triangle-free and has a specified fitting factorization $F_{ij}=\sum_{T\in\mathcal T}\alpha_i(T)\beta_j(T)$ over $\mathbb F_p$ with $t/m<1/100$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-op-graph"><summary>Proof of stmt-op-graph</summary>

*Proof.* Three pairwise adjacent sets would have union of size at least $3\cdot600-3\cdot199=1203$, exceeding $1200$. To construct the fit, let $\alpha_i(T)=1$ when $T\subseteq I_i$, and zero otherwise. Define $\beta_j(T)$ as the coefficient of $\prod_{x\in T}X_x$ obtained by expanding $$Q_j(X)=\prod_{k=200}^{599}
       \frac{\sum_{x\in I_j}X_x-k}{600-k}$$ and replacing every positive exponent in each monomial by one. All denominators are in $\{1,\ldots,400\}$, hence invertible. This replacement preserves evaluations on zero-one vectors and does not increase degree. Thus $$F_{ij}=\prod_{k=200}^{599}\frac{|I_i\cap I_j|-k}{600-k}.$$ It equals one for $i=j$. For distinct nonneighbors the intersection size lies between $200$ and $599$, so one factor is zero. This is the threshold-Kneser polynomial specialization of (Haviv 2018, Proposition 13) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Haviv2018Theta](#ref-Haviv2018Theta)] <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[GolovnevHaviv2022](#ref-GolovnevHaviv2022)] (Golovnev and Haviv 2022, Theorem 3.2).

The binomial coefficients increase up to index $400$. Consequently $$\frac{t}{m}
 \le401\prod_{u=401}^{600}\frac{u}{1201-u}
 \le401(5/7)^{100}<\frac1{100}.$$ In the second inequality the first hundred factors are at most $500/701<5/7$ and the rest are at most one. The last inequality follows from $(5/7)^3<1/2$ and $40100<2^{33}$. ◻

</details>

For the local algebra define the additive group $P=(\mathbb F_p)^3$, the function group $A_0=(\mathbb Z/M\mathbb Z)^P$, and $$H=A_0\rtimes P,\qquad
 (a,s)(a',s')=(a+s\cdot a',s+s'),\qquad
 (s\cdot a)(x)=a(x-s).$$ Its order is $w=M^{p^3}p^3$. Let $c\in A_0$ have value one at zero and zero elsewhere, and put $$u(s)=(0,s),\quad U=u(P),\qquad
 v(s)=cu(s)c^{-1},\quad V=cUc^{-1}.$$ The coordinate formula $v(s)=(c-s\cdot c,s)$ gives $U\cap V=\{1\}$, because $c-s\cdot c\ne0$ when $s\ne0$. We next construct an idempotent with incoming annihilation by $U$ and outgoing annihilation by $V$.

<a id="op:modular-section"></a>
### Large idempotents with ordered annihilations

The graph has supplied the ratio $t/m<1/100$. Our present objective is an idempotent in the finite group algebra $K[H]$ whose regular rank fraction exceeds $1/96$. It must also have two prescribed annihilations, on opposite sides. Here $$\ell=(p-1)/2,\qquad D=3(p-1),\qquad w=|H|.$$ The *regular rank* of $e\in K[H]$ means $\dim_K eK[H]$, the dimension of the image of left multiplication. Although this map multiplies on the left, its image is a right ideal.

<a id="stmt-op-modular"></a>
<div id="op:modular" class="proposition">

**Proposition 4**. *There is a specified idempotent $e\in K[H]$ satisfying, for every $s\in P$, $$(u(s)-1)^\ell e=0,\qquad e(v(s)-1)^\ell=0.$$ Its regular rank $d$ satisfies $d/w>1/96$. Its coefficients are obtained by finite operations in the specified field $K$.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### The annihilated column space

On $K^P$, let $\mathbf e_x$ be the standard basis and $L_s\mathbf e_x=\mathbf e_{s+x}$. For the three standard generators $\varepsilon_j$ of $P$ set $X_j=L_{\varepsilon_j}-I$. The $X_j$ commute, and characteristic $p$ gives $X_j^p=0$. The vectors $$X_1^{n_1}X_2^{n_2}X_3^{n_3}\mathbf e_0,
 \qquad 0\le n_1,n_2,n_3<p,$$ are a basis. Indeed, every translation of $\mathbf e_0$ is a product of powers of $I+X_j$ applied to $\mathbf e_0$, so the displayed family spans $K^P$; its cardinality is the dimension $p^3$.

<a id="op:B-annihilation"></a>
This is the elementary abelian augmentation filtration in the setting studied by Jennings (Jennings 1941) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Jennings1941](#ref-Jennings1941)]. Here the exact degree argument can be given directly. Let $B$ have as columns the displayed basis vectors with $n_1+n_2+n_3\ge D-\ell+1$, in order. For every $s$, the matrix $L_s-I$ is a polynomial in the $X_j$ with zero constant term. Multiplication by its $\ell$th power raises every column’s total degree to at least $D+1$. Such a monomial has an exponent at least $p$, so vanishes. Applying the same assertion to $-s$ and transposing yields $$\label{op:B-annihilation}
 (L_s-I)^\ell B=0,\qquad B^{\mathsf T}(L_s-I)^\ell=0.$$ The transpose identity uses $L_{-s}^{\mathsf T}=L_s$; no scalar conjugation is present.

The columns are independent. Their number $h$ can be counted by setting $a_j=p-1-n_j$: the new nonnegative indices have sum at most $\ell-1$. Their individual upper bounds are automatic because $\ell-1<p$. Stars and bars gives <a id="op:h"></a>$$\label{op:h}
 h=\binom{\ell+2}{3},\qquad
 \frac{h}{p^3}=\frac{(p-1)(p+1)(p+3)}{48p^3}>\frac1{48}.$$ For the strict inequality, the numerator minus $p^3$ is $3p^2-p-3>0$ for $p\ge3$.

The same subspace is killed on the two sides in Equation <a href="#op:B-annihilation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="op:B-annihilation">[op:B-annihilation]</a>, but an arbitrary projection onto it need not give the outgoing conjugate annihilation. We therefore choose the row factor by a diagonal weighted pairing.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Many nonsingular diagonal pairings

<a id="op:fourier"></a>
For a finite function group $A_*=(\mathbb Z/M\mathbb Z)^E$, its characters $\chi:A_*\to K^*$ are all exponent lists of powers of $\zeta$. Put $$\label{op:fourier}
 p_\chi^{A_*}=|A_*|^{-1}\sum_{a\in A_*}\chi(a)^{-1}a.$$ The denominator is invertible. Reindexing the sum gives $a p_\chi^{A_*}=\chi(a)p_\chi^{A_*}$. A nontrivial character has sum zero, since translating its sum by an element on which it is nontrivial multiplies it by a scalar different from one. It follows that $$p_\chi^{A_*}p_\psi^{A_*}=\delta_{\chi\psi}p_\chi^{A_*},\qquad
 \sum_\chi p_\chi^{A_*}=1.$$ For the last identity, sum coefficients over the independent character exponents: every nonzero coordinate of a nonidentity element is detected by a power of $\zeta$. Each idempotent is nonzero, there are $|A_*|$ of them, and orthogonality makes them independent. They form a basis of $K[A_*]$.

Specialize to $A_0$. Its character action is $(s\lambda)(a)=\lambda((-s)\cdot a)$. Define $$\chi_x=x\lambda,\qquad
 D_\lambda=\operatorname{diag}(\chi_x(c):x\in P),\qquad
 N_\lambda=B^{\mathsf T}D_\lambda^{-1}B.$$ Call $\lambda$ admissible if its stabilizer in $P$ is trivial and $N_\lambda$ is invertible. This condition is a finite test.

<a id="stmt-op-good-count"></a>
<div id="op:good-count" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5**. *More than half of the $|A_0|=M^{p^3}$ characters are admissible.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-op-good-count"><summary>Proof of stmt-op-good-count</summary>

*Proof.* For each nonzero $s\in P$, translation partitions $P$ into $p^2$ cycles of length $p$. A character fixed by $s$ has constant exponent on each cycle, so exactly $M^{p^2}$ characters are fixed. A union bound therefore bounds the fraction with nontrivial stabilizer by $$(p^3-1)M^{p^2-p^3}.$$

<a id="op:weighted-gram"></a>
Let $c_r$ be the coordinate generator at $r$. Since $\chi_x(c)=\lambda(c_{-x})$, the diagonal entries of $D_\lambda^{-1}$ range independently over $K^*$ as $\lambda$ ranges over all characters. For independent variables $\tau_x$, Cauchy–Binet gives $$\label{op:weighted-gram}
 Q(\tau):=\det\bigl(B^{\mathsf T}\operatorname{diag}(\tau_x)B\bigr)
 =\sum_{\substack{J\subseteq P\\|J|=h}}\det(B_J)^2\prod_{x\in J}\tau_x.$$ Here $B_J$ consists of the indicated rows in their fixed order. Some maximal minor is nonzero because $B$ has full column rank. The monomials for distinct $J$ are distinct, so $Q$ is nonzero of total degree $h$, even though the unweighted Gram matrix could be singular.

We use the polynomial grid estimate of Schwartz (Schwartz 1980, Lemma 1 and Corollary 1) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Schwartz1980](#ref-Schwartz1980)]; see also the related identity-testing method of Zippel (Zippel 1979) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Zippel1979](#ref-Zippel1979)]. A nonzero polynomial of total degree at most $a$ vanishes on at most an $a/M$ fraction of a product grid whose coordinate sets each have size $M$. To prove this, induct on the number of variables. If the degree in the last variable is $b$, its leading coefficient has degree at most $a-b$. It vanishes at at most an $(a-b)/M$ fraction of the earlier choices. For each remaining choice, the last-variable polynomial has at most $b$ roots, contributing at most $b/M$. The sum proves the bound; the constant case starts the induction.

Applying the estimate on $(K^*)^{p^3}$ to Equation <a href="#op:weighted-gram" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="op:weighted-gram">[op:weighted-gram]</a>, the singular-pairing fraction is at most $h/M$. Both excluded fractions are strictly smaller than $1/4$: $$\frac hM\le\frac{p^3}{p^4-1}<\frac14,\qquad
 (p^3-1)M^{-p^2(p-1)}\le\frac{p^3-1}{M}<\frac14.$$ The estimates use $p>600$; for example, $p^4-1-4p^3=p^3(p-4)-1>0$. Their union therefore excludes fewer than half of the characters. ◻

</details>

Select the first admissible character from each $P$-orbit containing one, and call the set of selected characters $\mathscr R$. Each selected orbit is free, of size $p^3$. These orbits cover all admissible characters, so <a id="op:orbit-count"></a>$$\label{op:orbit-count}
 |\mathscr R|>\frac{|A_0|}{2p^3}.$$ This argument does not need admissibility to be constant on an orbit.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Realizing the projections in the group algebra

Fix $\lambda\in\mathscr R$. For $x,y\in P$, define $$E_{xy}^{\lambda}=p_{x\lambda}^{A_0}u(x-y).$$ Conjugation gives $u(s)p_\chi^{A_0}u(s)^{-1}=p_{s\chi}^{A_0}$. Fourier orthogonality and freeness of the orbit therefore imply $$E_{xy}^{\lambda}E_{zt}^{\lambda}=\delta_{yz}E_{xt}^{\lambda}.$$ Indeed the Fourier product can survive only when $x\lambda=(x-y+z)\lambda$, which is equivalent to $y=z$. Products between different orbits vanish. The sum $q_\lambda=\sum_x p_{x\lambda}^{A_0}$ is central: it commutes with $A_0$ and its summands are permuted by $P$. The basis $p_{x\lambda}^{A_0}u(s)$ of its ideal is indexed equally by $(x,y)$ through $s=x-y$. Thus these units prove $$q_\lambda K[H]\cong\operatorname{Mat}_{p^3}(K).$$ This is an explicit character-orbit block, in the normal-subgroup representation setting of Clifford (Clifford 1937) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Clifford1937Announcement](#ref-Clifford1937Announcement)]. It does not assert semisimplicity of the entire modular algebra.

On this block, the elements $u(s)$, $c$, and $v(s)$ act respectively by $$L_s,\qquad D_\lambda,\qquad D_\lambda L_sD_\lambda^{-1}.$$ For example, left multiplication by $u(s)$ shifts row $x$ to row $s+x$, whereas $c$ multiplies row $x$ by $(x\lambda)(c)$.

Define a matrix and then a finite group-algebra element by <a id="op:explicit-e"></a>$$\label{op:explicit-e}
 T_\lambda=BN_\lambda^{-1}B^{\mathsf T}D_\lambda^{-1},\qquad
 e=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathscr R}\sum_{x,y\in P}
       (T_\lambda)_{xy}E_{xy}^{\lambda}.$$ The inverse exists by the selection criterion. Since $B^{\mathsf T}D_\lambda^{-1}B=N_\lambda$, we have $T_\lambda B=B$ and $T_\lambda^2=T_\lambda$. Its image is exactly $\operatorname{im}B$, of dimension $h$. Orthogonality of the blocks proves $e^2=e$; on the complementary ideal its component is zero.

<a id="op:regular-rank"></a>
We now compute the regular rank, rather than the smaller rank of a single representation matrix. In $\operatorname{Mat}_{p^3}(K)$, left multiplication by $T_\lambda$ has image the matrices whose $p^3$ columns all lie in $\operatorname{im}B$. Its image dimension is $hp^3$. Summing the selected blocks gives $$\label{op:regular-rank}
 d=hp^3|\mathscr R|,\qquad
 \frac d w=\frac{h|\mathscr R|}{|A_0|}
 >\frac{h}{2p^3}>\frac1{96}.$$

The incoming annihilation follows from $(L_s-I)^\ell T_\lambda=0$. For the outgoing annihilation, keep the factors in their actual order: $$\begin{split}
 B^{\mathsf T}D_\lambda^{-1}
     (D_\lambda L_sD_\lambda^{-1}-I)^\ell
 &=B^{\mathsf T}(L_s-I)^\ell D_\lambda^{-1}\\
 &=0.
 \end{split}$$ Equation <a href="#op:B-annihilation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="op:B-annihilation">[op:B-annihilation]</a> proves both assertions on every selected block. They also hold on the complementary ideal, where $e$ is zero. This proves Proposition <a href="#op:modular" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:modular">4</a>, including its finite prescription and the strict rank estimate.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### From opposite annihilations to an ordered zero product

Take copies $H_i$ of $H$. For each edge $i<j$, identify $v(1,\bar j,\bar j^2)$ in $H_i$ with $u(1,\bar i,\bar i^2)$ in $H_j$, where bars denote residues in $\mathbb F_p$. Let $G_0$ be the resulting free-product quotient, and let $e_i$ denote the image of $e$ from $H_i$. The injectivity of these copies is proved in Section <a href="#sec:gluing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:gluing">4</a>. The following ordered identity already follows from the local calculations and the quotient relations.

<a id="stmt-op-edge-zero"></a>
<div id="op:edge-zero" class="corollary">

**Corollary 6**. *For every edge $i<j$ of $\Gamma$, $e_i e_j=0$ in $K[G_0]$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-op-edge-zero"><summary>Proof of stmt-op-edge-zero</summary>

*Proof.* Let $y$ be its generator, initially of order $p$ on either local side, and put $X=y-1$. In the local cyclic group algebra $K[\langle y\rangle]=K[X]/(X^p)$, the annihilator of right multiplication by $X^\ell$ is precisely the ideal $(X^{p-\ell})$. Choose representatives of the left cosets $a\langle y\rangle$ in $H_i$; they give a free right $K[\langle y\rangle]$-basis of $K[H_i]$. Thus the outgoing identity $e_iX^\ell=0$, applied coefficientwise in this basis before taking images in the quotient, gives $e_i=qX^{p-\ell}$ for some local $q$. On the incoming side $X^\ell e_j=0$. Since $p-\ell=\ell+1$, $$e_i e_j=qX^{\ell+1}e_j=qX(X^\ell e_j)=0.$$ No factor is commuted across $X$, and no reverse ordered product is asserted. ◻

</details>

We have constructed the large regular rank and the edge vanishings. To compare them with the graph factorization, the remaining structural step must put exact rectangular realizations of all those ranks inside one nonzero ring.

<a id="sec:gluing"></a>
### Gluing the finite groups without collapsing them

The quotient $G_0$ of Section <a href="#op:modular-section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:modular-section">3</a> identifies a cyclic subgroup across each edge of $\Gamma$. It must retain every vertex group: otherwise a later nonzero element of a finite group algebra could disappear. We first prove a general injectivity criterion and then verify it for the groups used here. The local injectivity argument belongs to the $C(4)$–$T(4)$ theory of graphical complexes of groups (Prytuła 2024, sec. 8) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Prytula2024](#ref-Prytula2024)]. We give a direct proof using only labelled spherical maps and Euler’s formula.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### An injectivity criterion

<a id="stmt-lem-triangle-free-gluing"></a>
<div id="lem:triangle-free-gluing" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:gluing-short-word"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Triangle-free gluing). *Let $\Delta$ be a finite simple triangle-free graph. For each vertex $i$, let $D_i$ be a finite group. For each edge $\gamma=\{i,j\}$, let $C_\gamma$ be a nontrivial finite cyclic group, with injective homomorphisms $\iota_{\gamma,i}:C_\gamma\to D_i$ and $\iota_{\gamma,j}:C_\gamma\to D_j$. Assume the following condition at every vertex $i$. If $1\leq k\leq3$, the edges $\gamma_1,\ldots,\gamma_k$ are incident to $i$, and $s_a\in C_{\gamma_a}\setminus\{1\}$, then $$\label{eq:gluing-short-word}
 \iota_{\gamma_1,i}(s_1)\cdots
 \iota_{\gamma_k,i}(s_k)\neq1$$ whenever consecutive edge labels are distinct cyclically. For $k=1$ the distinctness condition is omitted; for $k\geq2$ it includes $\gamma_k\neq\gamma_1$. Then every natural homomorphism $D_i\to Q$ is injective, where $$Q=\left(*_{i\in V(\Delta)}D_i\right)
 \big/\left\langle\!\left\langle
 \iota_{\gamma,i}(s)\iota_{\gamma,j}(s)^{-1}:
 \gamma=\{i,j\},\ s\in C_\gamma
 \right\rangle\!\right\rangle.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-triangle-free-gluing"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-triangle-free-gluing</summary>

*Proof.* We first handle the subgroups generated by the incident cyclic groups. The diagram argument will rule out a relation that is nontrivial at one vertex but follows from the relations at all the vertices. At the end, a permutation action extends the conclusion to the full groups $D_i$.

Put $$D_i^0=\langle\iota_{\gamma,i}(C_\gamma):\gamma\ni i\rangle.$$ Choose a generator of each $C_\gamma$, and let $L$ have one generator $x_\gamma$ for each edge. Its defining relators are all words in the $x_\gamma^{\pm1}$ incident to a single vertex that evaluate to $1$ in that vertex group. There is a well-defined map $D_i^0\to L$: two words representing the same element of $D_i^0$ differ by one of these relators.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
###### A failed injection produces a spherical map.

Suppose a nonidentity element of $D_i^0$ maps to $1$ in $L$, and choose a word $w$ representing it. There is a finite sequence of words from $w$ to the empty word, each step inserting or deleting a defining relator, its inverse, or a free inverse pair. Indeed, membership in the normal closure of the relators expresses $w$ in the free group as a finite product of conjugates of relators; free insertions and cancellations reach such a product, and the conjugated relators can then be erased.

We describe the spherical maps needed to encode this sequence. A map is a finite connected graph drawn tamely on the sphere, allowing loops and parallel edges. An edge is either *neutral*, with value $1$, or is labelled by an edge $\gamma$ of $\Delta$. In the latter case its two oriented traversals, called darts, have mutually inverse values in $C_\gamma\setminus\{1\}$. Every face has a type $i\in V(\Delta)$ incident to every label in its boundary walk. Its boundary word is evaluated in $D_i$, using the specified embeddings. A face is *ordinary* when this evaluation is the identity. Call the map *exceptional* if precisely one face has nonidentity evaluation and all other faces have identity evaluation. Changing the starting point conjugates a boundary product, and reversing the walk inverts it, so identity versus nonidentity is well-defined.

Draw the successive words in horizontal levels of a rectangle, with each letter an oriented edge. Represent an empty level by a single neutral edge. In every intervening strip join corresponding vertices along the unchanged prefix and suffix by noncrossing neutral edges. At an insertion or deletion, join the single insertion vertex on the shorter path to the two endpoints of the inserted block on the longer path. The strip faces then read either a letter followed by its inverse, or the inserted relator, with neutral edges interspersed. If a level is empty, the adjacent nonempty word is the entire inserted or deleted block; one face between these two levels suffices. Assign the relator’s vertex type to its face, and either endpoint type of a letter’s label to an unchanged-letter face. All these faces evaluate to $1$. Include the neutral edges along the two vertical sides of the rectangle. The exterior face has word $w$ or $w^{-1}$, apart from neutral edges; give it type $i$. This connected drawing, viewed on the sphere, is exceptional.

We shall prove that no exceptional map exists. Choose one with the smallest possible number of edges. This number is positive: the only face of an isolated vertex has the empty word, which evaluates to $1$. The reductions below will make every vertex degree and every ordinary face length at least four away from a possible loop. Euler’s formula will then rule out both the loop-free case and the remaining one-loop case.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
###### Boundary walks and elementary reductions.

<a id="eq:gluing-euler"></a>
Fix an orientation of the sphere. At each vertex let $\sigma$ be the cyclic successor of outgoing darts and let $\alpha$ reverse a dart. Boundary walks are the cycles of $\sigma\alpha$: after traversing a dart, take the successor of its reverse. For a disconnected drawing we use each component’s own boundary walks, counting one empty walk for an isolated vertex. If a drawing has $v$ vertices, $e$ edges, $F$ such walks and $c$ components, then $$\label{eq:gluing-euler}
 v-e+F=2c.$$ For completeness, in a connected spherical drawing a nonbridge edge lies on a simple circuit. Its two sides are therefore on distinct boundary walks; deleting it merges those walks and preserves connectedness. Repeated deletion leaves a tree, which has one boundary walk and $e=v-1$. This proves the formula for a connected drawing; summing proves the displayed formula. The same walk rules follow by taking small vertex discs and thin edge bands.

A neutral nonloop edge can be contracted, with its neutral darts omitted from the boundary words. This preserves every face evaluation and contradicts minimality. For a neutral loop based at $b$, retain the closed side containing the exceptional face, then remove the loop. The retained graph is connected: each side of the loop attaches to the other only at $b$. The other dart slots on the retained side form an interval between the loop slots; closing that interval gives precisely the old walks on that side with the neutral loop dart removed. Thus their evaluations are unchanged. If only $b$ remains, its walk is empty and could not be exceptional. In every possible case a smaller exceptional map results. Hence every edge is nonneutral.

There are no bridges. To see this, delete a bridge with value $s$, leaving two components. Its incident face walk splits into words $W_1,W_2$, one in each component, possibly empty. Both receive the old face type. With suitable starting point and orientation, the old word was $$W_1sW_2s^{-1}.$$ If it was exceptional, at least one $W_a$ has nonidentity evaluation; retain that component. If it was ordinary, the evaluations of $W_1$ and $W_2$ are either both identity or both nonidentity. In the first case retain the component with the original exceptional face. In the second retain the component without that face: its new boundary walk is now its unique exceptional face. All choices give a smaller exceptional map.

Every edge consequently separates two distinct faces. Their types must also be distinct. Otherwise delete the edge and merge the faces, using their common type. If their words, starting at its opposite darts, are $sD$ and $s^{-1}E$, the merged word is $DE$, and <a id="eq:gluing-merge"></a>$$\label{eq:gluing-merge}
 DE=s^{-1}(sD)s(s^{-1}E).$$ Thus two ordinary faces merge to an ordinary face, whereas one ordinary and one exceptional face merge to an exceptional face. Again this would reduce the number of edges. The types across an edge labelled $\gamma$ are therefore exactly the two endpoints of $\gamma$.

There is at most one loop, and if it exists it bounds the exceptional face as a monogon. In fact, given a loop, keep its closed side *without* the exceptional face, including the loop and its base vertex, and erase the graph on the other side. All retained old faces are ordinary. The erased side becomes a monogon with the loop’s nonidentity value; either endpoint of its label supplies its type. This is another exceptional map. Minimality forces the erased side to have contained no other edges in the original drawing. That side was consequently already the exceptional monogon. This applies to every loop, and the one exceptional face cannot be a monogon for two different loops.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
###### Consecutive equal labels can be smoothed.

We have reduced to nonneutral, nonbridge edges with different face types on their two sides; the sole possible loop bounds the exceptional monogon. It remains to rule out consecutive nonloop darts with equal labels and then compare degrees with Euler’s formula. The remaining reduction ensures that the short-word hypothesis applies to face boundaries. Suppose a face $\Phi$ has consecutive nonloop darts $$e:a\longrightarrow b,\qquad f:b\longrightarrow d$$ with a common label $\gamma$ and respective values $\epsilon,\varphi$. The two darts belong to distinct edges: otherwise the edge would occur on both sides of $\Phi$ and would be a bridge. Let $X,Y$ be the faces containing $\bar e,\bar f$, respectively. They are distinct from $\Phi$ and have a common type $j$, namely the other endpoint of $\gamma$ from the type of $\Phi$. They may coincide with each other.

Remove the two edges and insert an edge $g:a\to d$ along their arcs, smoothed through the empty corner of $\Phi$ at $b$. Give it value $\epsilon\varphi$ and label $\gamma$, or make it neutral when that product is $1$. If $a=d$, this produces a loop. Keep all vertices, including any that become isolated. At $a,d$ the new darts occupy the old slots of $e,\bar f$; at $b$ delete the consecutive slots $\bar e,f$. These instructions specify the spherical embedding even when vertices or other boundary segments coincide. The walk of $\Phi$ replaces $ef$ by $g$ and has unchanged evaluation.

When $X\neq Y$, write $$X=\bar eD,\qquad Y=\bar fE,
 \quad D:a\to b,\quad E:b\to d.$$ The walks $D,E$ avoid the removed edges, so the new graph remains connected. The two opposite faces merge to the walk $\bar gDE$. All other walks, apart from the specified change in $\Phi$, are unchanged. Its evaluation in $D_j$ is <a id="eq:gluing-smooth-merge"></a>$$\label{eq:gluing-smooth-merge}
 (\epsilon\varphi)^{-1}DE
 =\varphi^{-1}(\epsilon^{-1}D)\varphi(\varphi^{-1}E).$$ Thus the merged face is ordinary when both old faces were ordinary, and exceptional when exactly one was exceptional. This yields a smaller exceptional map.

<a id="eq:gluing-smooth-split"></a>
The case $X=Y$ requires keeping track of components. Write $$X=\bar eD\bar fE,
 \qquad D:a\to d,\quad E:b\to b.$$ The slot rules replace this single walk by the two walks $$\label{eq:gluing-smooth-split}
 \bar gD\qquad\hbox{and}\qquad E.$$ One can read this directly from $\sigma\alpha$: the transition from $\bar g$ is the old transition from $\bar e$, and removal of the two slots at $b$ joins the end of $E$ to its beginning. If $E$ is empty, the old transitions were $e\mapsto f$ and $\bar f\mapsto\bar e$. Equivalently, $\sigma(\bar e)=f$ and $\sigma(f)=\bar e$, so $b$ had degree two and now becomes an isolated vertex with its stipulated empty face. If $D$ is empty, then $a=d$ and $\bar gD$ is the monogon of the new loop. Thus the description includes all empty cases. Figure <a href="#fig:gluing-smoothing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:gluing-smoothing">1</a> illustrates the local replacement and the two boundary walks; all other incidences are suppressed.

<figure id="fig:gluing-smoothing">

<figcaption>Smoothing two consecutive nonloop darts of one label. All other incidences are omitted.
