The opposite faces $X, Y$ may be the same face; in that case its boundary splits as shown below the local picture. The resulting drawing has two components, as the boundary-walk calculation shows. If $E$ is empty, its component is the isolated vertex $b$.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-gluing-smoothing"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:gluing-smoothing`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=.95,>=latex,every node/.style={font=\small}]
 \coordinate (a) at (-3.8,0);
 \coordinate (b) at (-2.4,1);
 \coordinate (d) at (-1,0);
 \draw[->] (a)--node[above left]{$e,\epsilon$}(b);
 \draw[->] (b)--node[above right]{$f,\varphi$}(d);
 \fill (a) circle (1.5pt) node[below]{$a$};
 \fill (b) circle (1.5pt) node[above]{$b$};
 \fill (d) circle (1.5pt) node[below]{$d$};
 \node at (-2.4,.25) {$\Phi$};
 \node at (-3.75,1.15) {$X$};
 \node at (-1.05,1.15) {$Y$};
 \draw[->,thick] (-.5,.5)--(.5,.5);
 \coordinate (aa) at (1,0);
 \coordinate (bb) at (2.4,1);
 \coordinate (dd) at (3.8,0);
 \draw[->] (aa) to[bend left=25] node[below]{$g,\epsilon\varphi$}(dd);
 \fill (aa) circle (1.5pt) node[below]{$a$};
 \fill (bb) circle (1.5pt) node[above]{$b$};
 \fill (dd) circle (1.5pt) node[below]{$d$};
 \node at (2.4,-.25) {$\Phi$};
 \node[draw,rounded corners,inner sep=4pt] (oldwalk) at (-2.5,-1.5)
   {$\bar eD\bar fE$};
 \node[draw,rounded corners,inner sep=4pt] (newwalk) at (1.3,-1.5)
   {$\bar gD$};
 \node[draw,rounded corners,inner sep=4pt] (otherwalk) at (3.1,-1.5)
   {$E$};
 \draw[->] (oldwalk)--node[above]{$X=Y$}(newwalk);
 \node[below=3pt] at (oldwalk.south) {one boundary};
 \node[below=3pt] at (2.2,-1.8) {two components};
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 12 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Local replacement diagram and boundary walk splitting.
Top left: Vertices $a$, $b$, and $d$. A directed edge goes from $a$ to $b$ labeled $e, \epsilon$, with face $X$ above. A directed edge goes from $b$ to $d$ labeled $f, \varphi$, with face $Y$ above. A region below both edges is labeled $\Phi$.
A horizontal arrow points to the top right diagram.
Top right: Vertices $a$ and $d$ connected by a directed edge from $a$ to $d$ labeled $g, \epsilon\varphi$, with face $\Phi$ below. Above the edge, vertex $b$ appears as an isolated vertex.
Bottom: On the left, a box containing $\bar{e} D \bar{f} E$ labeled below as "one boundary". A horizontal arrow labeled $X = Y$ points to the right to two boxes, one containing $\bar{g} D$ and the other containing $E$, labeled below as "two components".
```


Across the components, the total number of boundary walks increases by one, the edge count decreases by one, and the vertex count is unchanged. Formula <a href="#eq:gluing-euler" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gluing-euler">[eq:gluing-euler]</a> gives exactly two components. The new edge places $a,d$ in one of them. Every component must contain an endpoint of a removed edge, because the old graph was connected; hence $b$ is in the other component. In particular, the two walks in <a href="#eq:gluing-smooth-split" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gluing-smooth-split">[eq:gluing-smooth-split]</a> belong to different components.

Give both walks type $j$, and denote their evaluations by $$A=(\epsilon\varphi)^{-1}D,\qquad B=E.$$ The old word has evaluation $\epsilon^{-1}D\varphi^{-1}B$. Moving the first factor $\epsilon^{-1}D$ to the end is a cyclic change of starting point, so it preserves identity versus nonidentity. The resulting evaluation is <a id="eq:gluing-split-evaluation"></a>$$\label{eq:gluing-split-evaluation}
 (\varphi^{-1}B\varphi)A.$$ If $X$ was exceptional, at least one of $A,B$ is nonidentity; retain its component. If $X$ was ordinary, $A$ and $B$ are either both identity or both nonidentity. In the former case keep the component with the original exceptional face, whose evaluation is unchanged even if it is $\Phi$. In the latter keep the component without that face; its new boundary is its sole exception. This again gives a smaller exceptional map. We have proved that no face of the minimal map has consecutive nonloop darts with equal labels, including the last and first darts of its cyclic boundary walk.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
###### Euler’s formula rules out the minimal map.

Consider a vertex that is not the base of a loop. Reading the adjacent face types around it gives a closed walk in $\Delta$. Each incident edge crosses between its two different endpoint types, and successive edge labels are distinct by the smoothing reduction. Thus the walk has no immediate reversal. Its length is positive since the connected map has edges. A simple triangle-free graph has no such closed walk of length less than four, so this vertex has degree at least four.

An ordinary face not incident to a loop has a nonempty boundary walk. Indeed every boundary walk of a connected graph with at least one edge is a nonempty dart cycle; an empty walk occurs only for an isolated vertex. Its consecutive labels are distinct, and the short-word condition <a href="#eq:gluing-short-word" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gluing-short-word">[eq:gluing-short-word]</a> therefore forces its length to be at least four. Here and below a loop counts twice toward its vertex degree, and boundary lengths count dart occurrences.

<a id="eq:gluing-deficit"></a>
For our connected spherical map, Euler’s formula and the two degree sums give $$\label{eq:gluing-deficit}
 \sum_{v}(4-\deg v)+\sum_{F}(4-|\partial F|)=8.$$ Without a loop, only the exceptional face can contribute positively, and its nonempty walk contributes at most $3$. With a loop, its base vertex contributes at most $2$, the exceptional monogon contributes $3$, and the face on the other side of the loop contributes at most $2$: that face is ordinary and cannot have length one, since its sole letter would be the loop’s nonidentity value. There are no other loops, so every remaining vertex and face contributes at most zero. The total is at most $2+3+2=7$, again contradicting <a href="#eq:gluing-deficit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gluing-deficit">[eq:gluing-deficit]</a>. Consequently no exceptional map exists, and every $D_i^0\to L$ is injective.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
###### Extending to the full vertex groups.

Let $\Omega=L\times\mathbb N$, with $L$ acting by left multiplication on the first coordinate. The restriction to each embedded $D_i^0$ is free and has countably infinitely many orbits. The same is true of the left $D_i^0$-action on $D_i\times\mathbb N$. Pair their orbits and choose one point in each pair; this determines a $D_i^0$-equivariant bijection $D_i\times\mathbb N\to\Omega$. Transport the free left $D_i$-action along this bijection. Its restriction to $D_i^0$ is exactly the original $L$-action. Make this choice separately for each $i$. The resulting actions agree on every edge group because both copies of its chosen generator act as $x_\gamma\in L$. They therefore define an action of $Q$ on $\Omega$, faithful on every $D_i$. This proves the required injections. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Application to the modular construction

Recall from Section <a href="#op:finite-section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:finite-section">2</a> the group $H=A_0\rtimes P$, with $P=(\mathbb F_p)^3$ and $A_0=(\mathbb Z/M\mathbb Z)^P$. Its subgroups $U=u(P)$ and $V=cUc^{-1}$ satisfy $U\cap V=\{1\}$. The quotient $G_0$ identifies, for every edge $i<j$, the elements <a id="eq:gluing-embeddings"></a>$$\label{eq:gluing-embeddings}
 v(1,\bar j,\bar j^{2})\in H_i,
 \qquad u(1,\bar i,\bar i^{2})\in H_j,$$ where $\bar i$ denotes the residue of $i$ in $\mathbb F_p$. Both elements have order $p$, so the corresponding maps from $C_p$ are embeddings. We now verify the remaining local condition.

<a id="stmt-prop-first-gluing-injective"></a>
<div id="prop:first-gluing-injective" class="proposition">

**Proposition 8**. *Every natural map $H_i\to G_0$ is injective. In particular, the maps $K[H_i]\to K[G_0]$ induced on group algebras are injective.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-first-gluing-injective"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-first-gluing-injective</summary>

*Proof.* We verify the short-word hypothesis of Lemma <a href="#lem:triangle-free-gluing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:triangle-free-gluing">7</a>. At a fixed vertex, every incident edge subgroup lies either in $U$ or in $V$. Within either subgroup, the labels supplied by distinct neighbors $j$ correspond to the vectors $(1,\bar j,\bar j^{2})$; a nonidentity syllable corresponds to a nonzero scalar multiple of its vector. Any three with distinct residues are independent, because their determinant is, up to sign, $$(\bar j_2-\bar j_1)(\bar j_3-\bar j_1)
 (\bar j_3-\bar j_2)\neq0.$$ The residues are distinct since $p>m$. The analogous first two coordinates show independence for two vectors, and a single vector is nonzero. Thus a word of at most three nonidentity syllables with distinct labels, all in $U$ or all in $V$, cannot evaluate to $1$.

For a mixed word of length two or three, one of $U,V$ supplies exactly one syllable. After a cyclic permutation, a putative relation would equate that nonidentity syllable to a product of syllables from the other subgroup. This contradicts $U\cap V=\{1\}$. For lengths two and three, cyclically distinct consecutive labels are precisely distinct labels, so all required words have been covered. The graph $\Gamma$ is simple and triangle-free, and all groups in question are finite. The lemma proves the injections. The group-algebra assertion follows because distinct elements of an embedded subgroup remain distinct basis elements of $K[G_0]$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:sector"></a>
### Realizing the local ranks in a common central sector

An idempotent in a finite group algebra has a well-defined rank as a linear operator. We now turn each of these numerical ranks into an isomorphism between modules over one common ring. The enlargement of the group is needed because the different simple or modular summands of the original group algebra need not have the same rank fraction.

Here are the precise inputs to this step. Let $K$ be a field of characteristic $p$, let $M$ be a positive integer prime to $p$, and assume that $K$ contains an element $\zeta$ of order $M$. In the application, $M=p^4-1$ and $|K|=p^4$. Write $C_M=\mathbb Z/M\mathbb Z$ and put $$P=(\mathbb F_p)^3,\qquad A_0=(C_M)^P,\qquad
 H=A_0\rtimes P,\qquad w=|H|,$$ where $P$ acts on the functions in $A_0$ by translation. Suppose that a countable group $G_0$ contains specified copies $H_1,\ldots,H_m$ of $H$. For each $i$, let $e_i\in K[H_i]$ be an idempotent, and assume that the positive integer $$d=\dim_K e_iK[H_i]$$ is independent of $i$. All matrices below act on column vectors in free right modules, so their entries multiply coordinates on the left.

<a id="stmt-prop-sector-ranks"></a>
<div id="prop:sector-ranks" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:sector-rectangular"></a>
**Proposition 9**. *Under these assumptions, there is a group $G_1$ containing $G_0$ and a central element $z\in G_1$ of order $M$ with the following property. Set $$R=K[G_1],\qquad
 f=\frac1M\sum_{k=0}^{M-1}\zeta^{-k}z^k,\qquad
 S=fR,\qquad P_i=fe_i.$$ Then $f$ is a nonzero central idempotent. There are explicitly specified matrices $$J_i\in\operatorname{Mat}_{w\times d}(S),\qquad
 Y_i\in\operatorname{Mat}_{d\times w}(S)$$ satisfying $$\label{eq:sector-rectangular}
 Y_iJ_i=fI_d,\qquad J_iY_i=P_iI_w.$$ The entries of $J_i$ and $Y_i$ lie in the group algebra of a finite subgroup of $G_1$, multiplied by $f$. Their construction uses only finite group operations and linear algebra over $K$.*

</div>

We prove the proposition in three stages. First we attach the finite groups while preserving all the given embeddings. Next we describe their $f$-parts as matrix algebras over group algebras of elementary abelian $p$-groups. Finally, nilpotence of the corresponding augmentation ideals makes the ranks exact and produces the two rectangular matrices.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Attaching the finite groups

Let $z$ initially generate an independent copy of $C_M$, and write $Q_{\mathrm{base}}=\langle z\rangle\times G_0$. For each $i$, define $$B_i=(C_M)^{H_i},\qquad L_i=B_i\rtimes H_i,
 \qquad (h\cdot b)(x)=b(h^{-1}x).$$ The groups $B_i$ are written additively. Let $\delta_i\in B_i$ be the constant function of value $1\in C_M$. It has order $M$ and is fixed by $H_i$, so it is central in $L_i$. Moreover, $\langle\delta_i\rangle\cap H_i=1$. Thus $C_i=\langle z\rangle\times H_i$ embeds in both $Q_{\mathrm{base}}$ and $L_i$, with $(z^k,h)$ sent to $(k\delta_i,h)$ in the latter. Define $G_1$ to be the quotient of the free product $$Q_{\mathrm{base}}*L_1*\cdots*L_m$$ that identifies these two copies of $C_i$ for every $i$.

For completeness, the following action proves that all the factors embed in this quotient. Let $X$ be a countable disjoint union of copies of the left regular $Q_{\mathrm{base}}$-set. Its restriction to each $C_i$ is free and has countably infinitely many orbits. A countable disjoint union $X_i$ of regular $L_i$-sets has the same property when restricted to $C_i$. Pair their $C_i$-orbits and choose one point in each paired orbit; extending those point pairings equivariantly gives a $C_i$-equivariant bijection $X\to X_i$. Transport the regular $L_i$-action to $X$ using this bijection. The resulting actions of $Q_{\mathrm{base}},L_1,\ldots,L_m$ agree on every prescribed $C_i$, hence define an action of $G_1$ on $X$. Each factor acts faithfully, which proves its injectivity in $G_1$. This argument uses the assumed embeddings $H_i\hookrightarrow G_0$ in defining the subgroups $C_i\le Q_{\mathrm{base}}$.

The element $z$ is central in every generating factor of $G_1$ and therefore in $G_1$; its order is still $M$ because $Q_{\mathrm{base}}$ embeds. In $R$, direct multiplication gives $$zf=\zeta f,\qquad
 f^2=\frac1M\sum_{k=0}^{M-1}\zeta^{-k}z^kf=f.$$ The coefficient of the identity in $f$ is $M^{-1}\ne0$, since the elements $1,z,\ldots,z^{M-1}$ are distinct. Thus $f$ is a nonzero central idempotent, and $S=fR$ is a ring with identity $f$. Each $P_i$ is an idempotent in $fK[L_i]$. Also, any identity $e_ie_j=0$ already valid in $K[G_0]$ remains valid after multiplication by $f$ in $S$.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### The matrix blocks of the central sector

Fix $i$ for the moment. A character of $B_i$ means a homomorphism $\chi:B_i\to K^*$. If $b_x$ denotes the coordinate function with value $1$ at $x\in H_i$ and $0$ elsewhere, then every character is uniquely specified by exponents $a_x\in\mathbb Z/M\mathbb Z$ through $\chi(b_x)=\zeta^{a_x}$. Define $$p_\chi=\frac1{|B_i|}\sum_{b\in B_i}\chi(b)^{-1}b
 \quad\text{in }K[B_i].$$ Apply the Fourier calculation of Equation <a href="#op:fourier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="op:fourier">[op:fourier]</a> to the finite index set $H_i$. The denominator is invertible because $p\nmid M$. The elements $p_\chi$ are a basis of pairwise orthogonal idempotents whose sum is $1$, and $bp_\chi=\chi(b)p_\chi$.

<a id="eq:sector-fourier-f"></a>
Use the action $$(h\chi)(b)=\chi(h^{-1}\cdot b).$$ Then $hp_\chi h^{-1}=p_{h\chi}$. Since $z=\delta_i$ is fixed by $H_i$, the set $$\mathcal X_i=\{\chi:\chi(z)=\zeta\}$$ is $H_i$-invariant, and the Fourier formulas give $$\label{eq:sector-fourier-f}
 f=\sum_{\chi\in\mathcal X_i}p_\chi.$$ For each orbit in $\mathcal X_i$, choose one representative $\theta$ and put $T_\theta=\operatorname{Stab}_{H_i}(\theta)$.

<a id="stmt-lem-sector-stabilizers"></a>
<div id="lem:sector-stabilizers" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10**. *For each such $\theta$, the subgroup $T_\theta$ is elementary abelian of exponent $p$ and rank at most $3$, with the trivial subgroup allowed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-sector-stabilizers"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-sector-stabilizers</summary>

*Proof.* Write $\theta(b_x)=\zeta^{a_x}$. Since $z=\sum_{x\in H_i}b_x$, the condition on $\theta$ is $$\sum_{x\in H_i}a_x=1\quad\text{in }\mathbb Z/M\mathbb Z.$$ Suppose that a nonidentity $g\in A_0\cap T_\theta$ has order $q$. The exponent of $A_0$ divides $M$, so $q>1$ divides $M$. Left multiplication by $g$ partitions $H_i$ into cycles of length exactly $q$. Invariance of $\theta$ says that the exponents $a_x$ are constant on these cycles. Their sum consequently lies in $q(\mathbb Z/M\mathbb Z)$. It cannot equal $1$: reduction modulo $q$ would give $0=1$. This contradiction proves $T_\theta\cap A_0=1$. The quotient homomorphism $H_i\to H_i/A_0\cong P$ is therefore injective on $T_\theta$. Every subgroup of the additive group $(\mathbb F_p)^3$ is an $\mathbb F_p$-linear subspace, giving the assertion. ◻

</details>

Eliminating the prime-to-$p$ part of each stabilizer is what makes its augmentation ideal nilpotent, and will permit the later change of basis. We next give the block isomorphism, including its inverse. This is the character-orbit construction already used in Section <a href="#op:modular-section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:modular-section">3</a>, now allowing nontrivial stabilizers; compare the Clifford-theoretic orbit picture in (Clifford 1937) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[Clifford1937Announcement](#ref-Clifford1937Announcement)]. It will identify the underlying $K$-linear rank of $P_i$ in every block with the same original integer $d$. For one orbit representative $\theta$, abbreviate $$T=T_\theta,\qquad s=[H_i:T],\qquad D=K[T].$$ Choose representatives $r_1,\ldots,r_s$ of the left cosets $hT$. The space $K[H_i]$ is a free right $D$-module with this ordered basis. Let $g\in H_i$ act on it by left multiplication $L_g$, and let $b\in B_i$ act diagonally by $$D_b(h)=(h\theta)(b)h\qquad(h\in H_i).$$ Both actions commute with the right action of $T$. For the diagonal action this follows from $(ht)\theta=h\theta$ for $t\in T$. The semidirect-product relation is also respected, because $$L_gD_bL_g^{-1}(h)
   =((g^{-1}h)\theta)(b)h
   =(h\theta)(g\cdot b)h
   =D_{g\cdot b}(h).$$ Thus we have an algebra homomorphism $\rho_\theta:K[L_i]\to\operatorname{Mat}_s(D)$. The element $z$ acts as $\zeta$, so $\rho_\theta(f)=I_s$.

<a id="eq:sector-block-inverse"></a>
For $t_0\in T$ and $1\le a,b\le s$, consider $$\label{eq:sector-block-inverse}
 p_{r_a\theta}\,r_at_0r_b^{-1}.$$ It belongs to $fK[L_i]$ by <a href="#eq:sector-fourier-f" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-fourier-f">[eq:sector-fourier-f]</a>. In the $\theta$-representation it sends $r_bt'$ to $r_at_0t'$ for every $t'\in T$ and vanishes on every other basis coset. To check the last assertion, its left group factor sends the coset $r_cT$ to $r_at_0r_b^{-1}r_cT$; the Fourier projection keeps this coset precisely when it equals $r_aT$, which is equivalent to $c=b$. In a representation associated to another orbit, the Fourier projection vanishes identically. Consequently <a href="#eq:sector-block-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-block-inverse">[eq:sector-block-inverse]</a> maps to the matrix with entry $t_0$ in position $(a,b)$ of the $\theta$-block and zero elsewhere.

<a id="eq:sector-blocks"></a>
Taking the product of these representations gives a surjective map $$\label{eq:sector-blocks}
 \rho:fK[L_i]\longrightarrow
 \prod_{\theta}\operatorname{Mat}_{[H_i:T_\theta]}(K[T_\theta]).$$ It is injective as well. The elements $p_\chi h$, for $\chi\in\mathcal X_i$ and $h\in H_i$, form a basis of its source. An orbit of size $s$ contributes $sw=s^2|T|$ basis elements, equal to the dimension of the corresponding target block. Hence the source and target have equal finite $K$-dimension. Formula <a href="#eq:sector-block-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-block-inverse">[eq:sector-block-inverse]</a>, extended linearly in $t_0$ and summed over the positions and blocks, is an explicit inverse of $\rho$.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Turning dimensions into rectangular identities

<a id="eq:sector-regular-rank"></a>
Continue in one block and let $E=\rho_\theta(P_i)\in
\operatorname{Mat}_s(D)$. As a $K$-linear operator on $K[H_i]$, $E$ is exactly left multiplication by $e_i$, since $f$ acts as the identity. Therefore $$\label{eq:sector-regular-rank}
 \dim_K\operatorname{im}E=d.$$ This is the link between the original rank calculation and the block decomposition. We will now obtain a basis in which $E$ is a coordinate projection over $D$, not just over $K$. The argument is a constructive nilpotent-ideal instance of the freeness of projective modules over local rings, studied by Kaplansky (Kaplansky 1958, Theorem 2) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[Kaplansky1958](#ref-Kaplansky1958)]; no general projective-module theorem is needed here.

Let $\varepsilon:D\to K$ be augmentation, and set $\mathfrak a=\ker\varepsilon$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:sector-stabilizers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sector-stabilizers">10</a>, we may choose generators $t_1,\ldots,t_q$ of $T\cong(C_p)^q$ with $0\le q\le3$. The elements $X_j=t_j-1$ commute, satisfy $X_j^p=0$, and generate $\mathfrak a$. Expanding $t_j=1+X_j$ and counting dimensions gives $$D\cong K[X_1,\ldots,X_q]/(X_1^p,\ldots,X_q^p).$$ It follows that $$\mathfrak a^{D_{\max}+1}=0,\qquad D_{\max}=3(p-1).$$ Indeed, a monomial of total degree greater than $q(p-1)$ has an exponent at least $p$. This also covers $q=0$, when $D=K$ and $\mathfrak a=0$. Matrices with entries in $\mathfrak a$ have the same nilpotence bound under multiplication, because each entry of a product of $D_{\max}+1$ such matrices lies in $\mathfrak a^{D_{\max}+1}$.

<a id="eq:sector-Q-inverse"></a>
<a id="eq:sector-dimensions"></a>
Put $\overline E=\varepsilon(E)$, applying augmentation entrywise, and let $d_0=\operatorname{rank}_K\overline E$. As $\overline E$ is idempotent, its image and the image of $I_s-\overline E$ form complementary subspaces of $K^s$. Scan the columns of $\overline E$ in order and keep each column that increases their span; then do the same for $I_s-\overline E$. Build $Q$ by taking the corresponding $d_0$ columns of $E$, followed by the corresponding $s-d_0$ columns of $I_s-E$. Its augmentation $Q_0$ is invertible over $K$. Regard $Q_0$ as a scalar matrix over $D$, and set $N=Q_0^{-1}(Q-Q_0)$. Its entries belong to $\mathfrak a$, so $N^{D_{\max}+1}=0$. Since $Q=Q_0(I_s+N)$, $$\label{eq:sector-Q-inverse}
 Q^{-1}=\left(\sum_{k=0}^{D_{\max}}(-N)^k\right)Q_0^{-1}.$$ Each of the first $d_0$ columns of $Q$ is fixed by $E$, and every remaining column is killed by $E$. Thus, for $\Pi=\operatorname{diag}(I_{d_0},0)$, $$EQ=Q\Pi,\qquad Q^{-1}EQ=\Pi.$$ Conjugation by $Q$ preserves $K$-dimension of images. Comparing with <a href="#eq:sector-regular-rank" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-regular-rank">[eq:sector-regular-rank]</a> gives the integer equalities $$\label{eq:sector-dimensions}
 d=d_0|T|,\qquad w=s|T|,\qquad wd_0=sd.$$ In particular, these are equalities of ordinary dimensions, not equalities after reduction to $K$.

We can now match coordinates exactly. Order pairs lexicographically and choose the increasing bijection between $$\{(\mu,l):1\le\mu\le d,\ 1\le l\le s\}
 \quad\text{and}\quad
 \{(\lambda,k):1\le\lambda\le w,\ 1\le k\le d_0\};$$ the sets have equal size by <a href="#eq:sector-dimensions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-dimensions">[eq:sector-dimensions]</a>. Let $V$ be the $w$-by-$d$ block matrix with $s$-by-$s$ entries that sends coordinate $(\mu,l)$ to its paired coordinate $(\lambda,k)$. Thus $V_{\lambda\mu}$ is the sum of standard matrix units $E_{kl}^{\mathrm{std}}$ over all such pairings. Let $W$ be the $d$-by-$w$ block matrix reversing each pairing, with $W_{\mu\lambda}$ the corresponding sum of $E_{lk}^{\mathrm{std}}$. It sends all unpaired coordinates with $k>d_0$ to zero. Directly on these coordinates, $$WV=I_d\otimes I_s,\qquad VW=I_w\otimes\Pi.$$ Define block matrices $$J^{(\theta)}=(I_w\otimes Q)V,\qquad
 Y^{(\theta)}=W(I_w\otimes Q^{-1}).$$ They have outer shapes $w\times d$ and $d\times w$, respectively, with entries in $\operatorname{Mat}_s(D)$. Their products are $$Y^{(\theta)}J^{(\theta)}=I_d\otimes I_s,\qquad
 J^{(\theta)}Y^{(\theta)}=I_w\otimes E.$$ Finally define the $(\lambda,\mu)$ entry of $J_i$ by applying $\rho^{-1}$ to the tuple $(J^{(\theta)}_{\lambda\mu})_\theta$, and similarly define $Y_i$ entrywise from the $Y^{(\theta)}$. The isomorphism <a href="#eq:sector-blocks" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-blocks">[eq:sector-blocks]</a> carries $f$ to the tuple of identity matrices and $P_i$ to the tuple of matrices $E$. The last two identities therefore pull back to <a href="#eq:sector-rectangular" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-rectangular">[eq:sector-rectangular]</a>. This proves Proposition <a href="#prop:sector-ranks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:sector-ranks">9</a>.

All choices in the matrix construction are over finite sets: character orbits, coset representatives, augmentation matrices, and coordinate pairings. Taking the first element or the first independent columns in fixed orders makes the prescription definite. The inverse <a href="#eq:sector-Q-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-Q-inverse">[eq:sector-Q-inverse]</a> is a finite sum, and the inverse <a href="#eq:sector-block-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-block-inverse">[eq:sector-block-inverse]</a> is a finite group-algebra formula. Thus the matrices furnished by the proposition are finite sums in the explicitly attached finite groups.

We now apply Proposition <a href="#prop:sector-ranks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:sector-ranks">9</a> to the embedded groups of Proposition <a href="#prop:first-gluing-injective" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:first-gluing-injective">8</a> and the identical idempotents of Proposition <a href="#op:modular" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:modular">4</a>. The group $G_0$ is countable because it is finitely generated. All the ranks equal the positive integer $d$ from Equation <a href="#op:regular-rank" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="op:regular-rank">[op:regular-rank]</a>. Hence the group $G_1$, the nonzero central idempotent $f$, and the matrices $J_i,Y_i$ are now fixed by the constructions above. Corollary <a href="#op:edge-zero" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:edge-zero">6</a> also gives $P_iP_j=0$ on every edge with $i<j$. These are precisely the inputs to the next section.

<a id="op:compression-section"></a>
### Compression with a visible nonzero defect

We now use only the graph factorization and the precise common-sector output. The first lemma is independent of the group construction. Its proof explains why ordered edge annihilations, rather than symmetric orthogonality, suffice.

<a id="stmt-op-compression"></a>
<div id="op:compression" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11** (Triangular compression). *Let $S$ be a nonzero unital algebra over a field $K$, and let $m,t,w,d$ be positive integers. Suppose $P_i\in S$ are idempotents and $\alpha_i(T),\beta_j(T)\in K$ satisfy $$F_{ij}=\sum_{T=1}^t\alpha_i(T)\beta_j(T),\qquad
 F_{ii}=1,\qquad F_{ij}P_iP_j=0\quad(i<j).$$ Suppose there are $J_i\in\operatorname{Mat}_{w\times d}(S)$ and $Y_i\in\operatorname{Mat}_{d\times w}(S)$ with $Y_iJ_i=I_d$, $J_iY_i=P_iI_w$. Then explicit matrices $$C_\sharp\in\operatorname{Mat}_{wt\times md}(S),\qquad
 D_\sharp\in\operatorname{Mat}_{md\times wt}(S)$$ satisfy $D_\sharp C_\sharp=I_{md}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-op-compression"><summary>Proof of stmt-op-compression</summary>

*Proof.* Define $C_0$ of shape $t\times m$ and $D_0$ of shape $m\times t$ by $$(C_0)_{T,j}=\beta_j(T)P_j,\qquad
 (D_0)_{i,T}=\alpha_i(T)P_i.$$ Their product has entries $F_{ij}P_iP_j$. Therefore $D_0C_0=\mathcal P+\mathcal N$, where $\mathcal P=\operatorname{diag}(P_1,\ldots,P_m)$ and $\mathcal N$ is strictly lower triangular. In particular $\mathcal N^m=0$. Every off-diagonal entry begins with $P_i$ and ends with $P_j$, so idempotence gives $$\mathcal P^2=\mathcal P,\qquad
 \mathcal P\mathcal N=\mathcal N=\mathcal N\mathcal P.$$ This is all the commutation information needed. The finite sum $$\mathcal Q=\mathcal P+\sum_{k=1}^{m-1}(-\mathcal N)^k$$ satisfies $\mathcal Q(\mathcal P+\mathcal N)=\mathcal P$ by cancellation of consecutive powers. Put $D_1=\mathcal QD_0$; then $D_1C_0=\mathcal P$. For $m=1$ the sum is empty and the same identity holds.

Take block diagonals $C_{\rm rep}$ and $D_{\rm rep}$ of $w$ copies of $C_0$ and $D_1$. Use ordered coordinates $(\lambda,i)$ on $S^{wm}$ and $(\lambda,T)$ on $S^{wt}$. Their product is $$D_{\rm rep}C_{\rm rep}=\mathcal P_{\rm rep}
 :=\operatorname{diag}_{\lambda=1}^w(\mathcal P).$$ Define matrices $\mathcal J:S^{md}\to S^{wm}$ and $\mathcal Y:S^{wm}\to S^{md}$ by $$\mathcal J_{(\lambda,i),(j,\mu)}
     =\delta_{ij}(J_i)_{\lambda\mu},\qquad
 \mathcal Y_{(j,\mu),(\lambda,i)}
     =\delta_{ij}(Y_i)_{\mu\lambda},
 \quad 1\le\mu\le d.$$ The assumed rectangular identities give $\mathcal Y\mathcal J=I_{md}$ and $\mathcal J\mathcal Y=\mathcal P_{\rm rep}$. Thus $$C_\sharp=C_{\rm rep}\mathcal J,\qquad
 D_\sharp=\mathcal YD_{\rm rep}$$ have the asserted shapes and satisfy $$D_\sharp C_\sharp
 =\mathcal Y\mathcal P_{\rm rep}\mathcal J
 =\mathcal Y\mathcal J\mathcal Y\mathcal J=I_{md}.$$ Only associativity and the stated identities have been used; the $P_i$ are not assumed to commute. ◻

</details>

Apply the lemma to $S=fK[G_1]$, whose identity is $f$. Distinct nonedges have $F_{ij}=0$ by Proposition <a href="#op:graph" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:graph">3</a>; edges with $i<j$ have $P_iP_j=0$. The rectangular identities are exactly those of Proposition <a href="#prop:sector-ranks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:sector-ranks">9</a>. Consequently <a id="op:split"></a>$$\label{op:split}
 D_\sharp C_\sharp=fI_{md},\qquad
 C_\sharp:S^{md}\longrightarrow S^{wt}.$$ The strict, ordinary rational inequalities are $$\frac t m<\frac1{100}<\frac1{96}<\frac d w,\qquad
 r:=wt<md=:N.$$ This is more than an abstract rank paradox: it gives a nonzero vector that will certify the reverse-product defect.

<a id="stmt-op-kernel"></a>
<div id="op:kernel" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12** (A square inverse and its certificate). *Let $R$ be a unital ring, $f\ne0$ a central idempotent, and $S=fR$. Suppose $N>r\ge1$ and matrices $C\in\operatorname{Mat}_{r\times N}(S)$, $D\in\operatorname{Mat}_{N\times r}(S)$ satisfy $DC=fI_N$. Let $\iota:S^r\to S^N$ include the first $r$ coordinates and $\pi:S^N\to S^r$ retain them, with entries $f$ and zero. Then $$A_R=\pi D+(1-f)I_r,\qquad B_R=C\iota+(1-f)I_r$$ satisfy $A_RB_R=I_r$ and $B_RA_R\ne I_r$. More precisely, if $u_*\in S^N$ has last coordinate $f$ and all others zero, then $$v_*:=Cu_*\ne0,\qquad Dv_*=u_*,\qquad
 A_Rv_*=0,\qquad (B_RA_R-I_r)v_*=-v_*\ne0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-op-kernel"><summary>Proof of stmt-op-kernel</summary>

*Proof.* Every entry of $C,D,\iota,\pi$ belongs to $S$ and is annihilated on both sides by $1-f$. Since $\pi\iota=fI_r$, $$A_RB_R=\pi DC\iota+(1-f)I_r=fI_r+(1-f)I_r=I_r.$$ The vector $u_*$ is nonzero because $f\ne0$. The equation $Dv_*=DCu_*=u_*$ proves $v_*\ne0$. But $N>r$ makes $\pi u_*=0$, so $$A_Rv_*=\pi Dv_*+(1-f)v_*=0.$$ Multiplying by $B_R$ and subtracting $v_*$ gives the final displayed identity and proves the strict reversal defect. ◻

</details>

Using $C=C_\sharp$, $D=D_\sharp$ in Lemma <a href="#op:kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:kernel">12</a> gives square matrices over the entire algebra $K[G_1]$, not only the $f$-summand. The nonzero certificate is rooted in the exact order of $z$, which proves $f\ne0$, and then in $D_\sharp v_*=u_*$. It does not depend on identifying a coefficient by deciding equality of group words. The remaining step is to retain this defect when replacing matrices by scalar elements.

<a id="op:scalar-section"></a>
### An injective finite-group encoding

We have a strict matrix one-sided inverse over $K[G_1]$. The finite-factor reduction of Dykema and Juschenko (Dykema and Juschenko 2015, Lemma 2.1 and Theorem 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[DykemaJuschenko2015](#ref-DykemaJuschenko2015)] explains the appropriate next step: put a full matrix algebra inside a finite group algebra and fill the complementary summand with the identity. We prove the actual matrix block needed here, including its injectivity. Its shift and diagonal operators are the finite Heisenberg model; the comparison with (Gurevich and Hadani 2009, sec. 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[GurevichHadani2009](#ref-GurevichHadani2009)] is methodological, since our cyclic order can be composite and our coefficient field is finite.

<a id="stmt-op-heisenberg"></a>
<div id="op:heisenberg" class="proposition">

**Proposition 13**. *Let $K$ be a field, let $M\ge2$ be invertible in $K$, and let $\zeta\in K$ have order $M$. For an integer $q\ge0$ put $Z_q=(\mathbb Z/M\mathbb Z)^q$ and $n=M^q$. There is a finite group $\Lambda$ of order $Mn^2$ and a nonzero central idempotent $f'\in K[\Lambda]$ for which explicitly specified elements $\Omega_{uv}$, $u,v\in Z_q$, form a basis of $f'K[\Lambda]$ and obey $$\Omega_{uv}\Omega_{st}=\delta_{vs}\Omega_{ut},\qquad
 \sum_u\Omega_{uu}=f'.$$ Thus $\operatorname{Mat}_n(K)\cong f'K[\Lambda]$ by its standard matrix units.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-op-heisenberg"><summary>Proof of stmt-op-heisenberg</summary>

<a id="op:heisenberg-law"></a>
*Proof.* Use the set $\Lambda=Z_q\times Z_q\times\mathbb Z/M\mathbb Z$ with $$\label{op:heisenberg-law}
 (x,y,k)(x',y',k')=(x+x',y+y',k+k'+y\cdot x').$$ All coordinates and the dot product use residues modulo $M$. Associativity follows from the bilinear identity $$y\cdot x'+(y+y')\cdot x''=y'\cdot x''+y\cdot(x'+x'').$$ The zero tuple is the identity, and the inverse is $(-x,-y,-k+y\cdot x)$. No primality of $M$ is involved. The element $z'=(0,0,1)$ is central of exact order $M$. Define $$f'=\frac1M\sum_{k=0}^{M-1}\zeta^{-k}(z')^k.$$ Reindexing gives $z'f'=\zeta f'$, and convolution gives $(f')^2=f'$. The identity coefficient $M^{-1}$ is nonzero, so $f'\ne0$.

<a id="op:heisenberg-D"></a>
Write $X_x=(x,0,0)$ and $Y_y=(0,y,0)$, and put $$\label{op:heisenberg-D}
 D_v=\frac1n\sum_{y\in Z_q}\zeta^{-y\cdot v}f'Y_y.$$ The denominator is invertible because $n=M^q$. Character orthogonality here means the direct identity <a id="op:composite-orthogonality"></a>$$\label{op:composite-orthogonality}
 \sum_{y\in Z_q}\zeta^{y\cdot a}
 =\begin{cases}n,&a=0,\\0,&a\ne0.\end{cases}$$ When $a\ne0$, choose a nonzero coordinate $a_j$. Translation of the sum by the $j$th coordinate vector multiplies it by $\zeta^{a_j}\ne1$. In the field $K$, this forces the sum to vanish. The argument still works when $a_j$ is a zero divisor modulo the composite integer $M$.

Convolution in the abelian group of $Y_y$ gives $D_vD_t=\delta_{vt}D_t$; indeed the coefficient of $f'Y_b$ in the product is $$\frac{\zeta^{-b\cdot t}}{n^2}
       \sum_{y\in Z_q}\zeta^{y\cdot(t-v)}.$$ Summing Equation <a href="#op:heisenberg-D" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="op:heisenberg-D">[op:heisenberg-D]</a> over $v$ also gives $\sum_vD_v=f'$. The group law gives $Y_yX_a=(z')^{y\cdot a}X_aY_y$, and hence $$D_vX_a=X_aD_{v-a}.$$ Set <a id="op:matrix-units"></a>$$\label{op:matrix-units}
 \Omega_{uv}=X_{u-v}D_v
 =X_{u-v}\frac1n\sum_{y\in Z_q}\zeta^{-y\cdot v}f'Y_y.$$ Then $$\Omega_{uv}\Omega_{st}
 =X_{u-v+s-t}D_{v-s+t}D_t
 =\delta_{vs}\Omega_{ut},$$ and the diagonal sum is $f'$.

The multiplication identities alone do not yet prove that this block has no kernel. On $K^{Z_q}$ define $$\rho(x,y,k)\mathbf e_s=\zeta^{k+y\cdot s}\mathbf e_{s+x}.$$ The scalar in a composite is $\zeta^{k+k'+y\cdot x'+(y+y')\cdot s}$, so Equation <a href="#op:heisenberg-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="op:heisenberg-law">[op:heisenberg-law]</a> proves that $\rho$ is a representation. It sends $f'$ to the identity and $D_v$ to the coordinate projection at $v$. Therefore $$\rho(\Omega_{uv})\mathbf e_s=\delta_{vs}\mathbf e_u,$$ which proves independence of all $n^2$ elements $\Omega_{uv}$. On the other hand, the $n^2$ elements $f'(x,y,0)$ span $f'K[\Lambda]$, because $f'(x,y,k)=\zeta^k f'(x,y,0)$. Their group-basis supports have distinct $(x,y)$ coordinates, so they too are independent. The sector has dimension exactly $n^2$, and the matrix units form a basis. When $q=0$, the empty tuple set has one element: $n=1$, $\Lambda=C_M$, and the sole matrix unit is $f'$. All formulas remain valid. ◻

</details>

The elementary identity-completion step is worth recording separately. Its unit need not be central in the target ring.

<a id="stmt-op-complement"></a>
<div id="op:complement" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14**. *Let $\Phi:\mathcal A\to\mathcal B$ be an injective ring homomorphism between unital rings, not necessarily preserving their identities, and put $g=\Phi(1_{\mathcal A})$. If $AB=1_{\mathcal A}$ and $BA\ne1_{\mathcal A}$, then $$a=1_{\mathcal B}-g+\Phi(A),\qquad
 b=1_{\mathcal B}-g+\Phi(B)$$ satisfy $ab=1_{\mathcal B}$ and $ba\ne1_{\mathcal B}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-op-complement"><summary>Proof of stmt-op-complement</summary>

*Proof.* The element $g$ is idempotent and is an identity on the image of $\Phi$ on both sides. Thus $1-g$ annihilates that image on both sides. Expanding the two products gives $$ab=1-g+\Phi(AB)=1,\qquad
 ba-1=\Phi(BA-1)\ne0.$$ Only the last inequality uses injectivity; no global centrality of $g$ is required. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-op-scalar"></a>
<div id="op:scalar" class="corollary">

**Corollary 15**. *Under the field hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#op:heisenberg" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:heisenberg">13</a>, suppose $A,B\in\operatorname{Mat}_r(K[L])$ for a group $L$ and an integer $r\ge1$, with $AB=I_r$ and $BA\ne I_r$. Finite formulas give a finite group $\Lambda$ and $a,b\in K[L\times\Lambda]$ with $ab=1$ and $ba\ne1$. In particular, over any fixed finite field with at least three elements, a failure of stable finiteness transfers to a scalar failure after a finite direct factor, without changing the field.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-op-scalar"><summary>Proof of stmt-op-scalar</summary>

*Proof.* Choose the least $q\ge0$ with $n=M^q\ge r$. This search is finite, since $M^r\ge2^r\ge r$. Pad the matrices by identities: $$\widetilde A=\operatorname{diag}(A,I_{n-r}),\qquad
 \widetilde B=\operatorname{diag}(B,I_{n-r}).$$ The padding is empty if $n=r$. Then $\widetilde A\widetilde B=I_n$ and $\widetilde B\widetilde A-I_n=\operatorname{diag}(BA-I_r,0)\ne0$. Use Proposition <a href="#op:heisenberg" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:heisenberg">13</a> and the group-basis identification $K[L\times\Lambda]=K[L]\otimes_K K[\Lambda]$. For an $n$-square matrix $T$ over $K[L]$, put $$\Phi(T)=\sum_{u,v\in Z_q}T_{uv}\otimes\Omega_{uv}.$$ The matrix-unit products prove multiplicativity, with $\Phi(I_n)=1\otimes f'$. Independence of the $\Omega_{uv}$ gives injectivity: extend their coordinate dual functionals to $K[\Lambda]$ and apply each in the second tensor factor to recover $T_{uv}$. This works even when $K[L]$ has zero divisors.

<a id="op:scalar-formulas"></a>
Lemma <a href="#op:complement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:complement">14</a> now yields the promised finite sums $$\label{op:scalar-formulas}
 \begin{split}
 a&=1\otimes(1-f')+
       \sum_{u,v\in Z_q}\widetilde A_{uv}\otimes\Omega_{uv},\\
 b&=1\otimes(1-f')+
       \sum_{u,v\in Z_q}\widetilde B_{uv}\otimes\Omega_{uv}.
 \end{split}$$ Their full scalar product is one, and their reverse defect is $\Phi(\widetilde B\widetilde A-I_n)\ne0$. For a finite field of order $Q\ge3$, take $M=Q-1$ and a generator of its multiplicative group. Then $M\ge2$ is invertible in the field, so the preceding construction applies. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-op-scalar-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-op-scalar</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#op:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:main">1</a>.* Lemma <a href="#op:field" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:field">2</a> supplies the specified odd prime and field of order $p^4$. Propositions <a href="#op:graph" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:graph">3</a> and <a href="#op:modular" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:modular">4</a> give the graph fit and the strict regular-rank comparison. Proposition <a href="#prop:first-gluing-injective" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:first-gluing-injective">8</a> embeds the local groups in $G_0$, and Proposition <a href="#prop:sector-ranks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:sector-ranks">9</a> places their ranks in the nonzero ring $fK[G_1]$ with the required rectangular identities. Corollary <a href="#op:edge-zero" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:edge-zero">6</a> supplies every ordered edge product. Lemmas <a href="#op:compression" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:compression">11</a> and <a href="#op:kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:kernel">12</a> produce $A_R,B_R\in\operatorname{Mat}_r(K[G_1])$, with their nonzero certificate. Corollary <a href="#op:scalar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="op:scalar">15</a>, using $L=G_1$ and $M=p^4-1$, gives $G=G_1\times\Lambda$ and the sums in Equation <a href="#op:scalar-formulas" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="op:scalar-formulas">[op:scalar-formulas]</a>. Their product is one and their reverse-product defect is nonzero by the injectivity of the matrix encoding.

The group $G_0$ is generated by the finite groups $H_i$. The construction of $G_1$ adds only $\langle z\rangle$ and the finitely many finite groups $L_i$, and $\Lambda$ is finite. Thus $G$ is finitely generated. The embedded copy of any $H_i$ supplies nontrivial torsion. All field, group and coefficient choices were given by finite formulas and fixed orders, as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
###### Finite prescriptions and scope.

Every choice of character, basis, field element, or finite inverse occurs in an explicitly finite set. Products of the resulting group words define finite group-algebra sums even when different words happen to coincide. No word comparisons are needed to define the elements, and the kernel certificate and injective matrix block certify the nonzero reverse defect. The countable permutation actions used to certify group embeddings are not coefficient-search inputs.

Containing torsion does not say that every element has finite order. The group must be infinite, since finite-dimensional left multiplication proves direct finiteness for every finite group algebra, in modular characteristic as well as otherwise. The coefficient field throughout remains the specified $K$ of order $p^4$.

More generally, the supports of any two finite sums in a group algebra generate a finitely generated subgroup. Its group algebra embeds by the group bases, preserving both a one-sided product and its nonzero reverse defect. Thus a universal direct-finiteness theorem for finitely generated groups would already imply the assertion for all groups.

<a id="op:cellular-section"></a>
### The cellular automaton on all configurations

The final transfer is short and characteristic-independent. We give it locally to fix the multiplication order and the meaning of a missing configuration. Its group-ring ancestry appears in (Elek and Szabó 2004, sec. 1) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[ElekSzabo2004](#ref-ElekSzabo2004)] and the linear theory of (Ceccherini-Silberstein and Coornaert 2006) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[CSCCoornaert2006](#ref-CSCCoornaert2006)].

<a id="op:cellular-product"></a>
For any field $k$, group $L$, and finite sum $c=\sum_uc_u u\in k[L]$, define on all functions $x:L\to k$ $$(T_cx)(g)=\sum_uc_u x(gu).$$ Only the coefficient sum is finite; $x$ may have infinite support. Expanding the two finite sums gives $$\label{op:cellular-product}
 (T_cT_dx)(g)=\sum_{u,v}c_ud_vx(guv)=(T_{cd}x)(g).$$ Thus $T_cT_d=T_{cd}$ and $T_1=\mathrm{id}$. If $\delta_h$ is the point indicator at $h$, then $(T_c\delta_h)(1_L)=c_h$, which also proves faithfulness.

<a id="stmt-op-cellular"></a>
<div id="op:cellular" class="proposition">

**Proposition 16**. *Let $k$ be a finite field, $L$ a group, and $a,b\in k[L]$ with $ab=1$. Then $T_b:k^L\to k^L$ is an injective cellular automaton, with $$\operatorname{im}T_b=\{y\in k^L:T_{ba}y=y\}.$$ For every $h$ with $(ba-1)_h\ne0$, the configuration $\delta_h$ is absent from its image.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-op-cellular"><summary>Proof of stmt-op-cellular</summary>

*Proof.* The support of $b$ is a finite memory set with rule $(x_u)_u\mapsto\sum_u b_ux_u$. Equation <a href="#op:cellular-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="op:cellular-product">[op:cellular-product]</a> gives $T_aT_b=T_{ab}=\mathrm{id}$, proving injectivity. If $y=T_bx$, then $T_{ba}y=T_bT_aT_bx=y$. Conversely, a fixed point of $T_{ba}$ has $y=T_b(T_ay)$, hence lies in the image. Finally, $$(T_{ba}\delta_h-\delta_h)(1_L)=(ba-1)_h\ne0,$$ so $\delta_h$ is not a fixed point and cannot be in the image. ◻

</details>

Apply the proposition to the scalar sums in Equation <a href="#op:scalar-formulas" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="op:scalar-formulas">[op:scalar-formulas]</a>. Their nonzero defect has a nonzero coefficient in the group basis, so the resulting automaton is injective and nonsurjective. The missing point indicator has no preimage even among infinitely supported configurations. If the finite formula for $b$ displays a group element more than once, its terms simply add in the same local rule; specifying the rule requires no word-problem oracle. Thus the constructed group also gives a negative answer to Gottschalk’s surjunctivity conjecture.
